<meta>This page serves as the introductory disclaimer and usage guide for a digitized volume, establishing the legal and functional context for the reader.</meta>

->Google<-

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the <term>public domain</term> <note>Works no longer protected by intellectual property laws and free for public use.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other <term>marginalia</term> <note>Notes or marks written in the margins of a book by previous readers or librarians.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ ***Make non-commercial use of the files*** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
+ ***Refrain from automated querying*** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
+ ***Maintain attribution*** The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
+ ***Keep it legal*** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

# I
## SURVEY OF THE MANUSCRIPTS

**C** PARISINVS COISLINIANVS 322 <note>A Greek manuscript held in the Coislin collection of the National Library of France.</note> (cf. H. Omont, *Summary Inventory of the Greek Manuscripts of the National Library* III p. 182) square format, 23.5 $\times$ 18 cm, parchment, from the end of the 11th or beginning of the 12th century $^{1}$), consisting of 402 leaves: one gathering of three sheets <term>trinion: a gathering of three folded sheets of parchment</term> marked $\bar{\epsilon}$ (leaves 1—6), followed by 49 gatherings of four sheets <term>quaternions: the standard medieval notebook structure of four folded sheets</term> ($\bar{\zeta}$—$\bar{\nu\delta}$ leaves 7—397 [though the number 135 is missing, 281 appears twice; in gathering $\bar{\nu\beta}$ the fifth leaf has been cut out]) and $\bar{\nu\epsilon}$ (leaves 398 and 399, to which three blank leaves are attached). Each page contains twenty-seven or twenty-eight lines of approximately 25 letters.

It contains the first and second books, though even these are not complete: the missing gatherings $\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}\bar{\gamma}\bar{\delta}$ <note>The first four gatherings of the original manuscript.</note> correspond exactly to the lost text up to page 32, line 2 "rather that |" <note>original: "ἐκεῖνο μᾶλλον |"</note>; the reason I mention this will become clear from the description of the next book (p. VIII). Furthermore, text has fallen out on leaf $116^{r}$ from page 150, line 19 "| first the" <note>original: "πρῶτον μὲν τὸ"</note> up to page 156, line 11 "foresight in everything |" <note>original: "παντὶ προμηθίαν |"</note>; and between gatherings $\bar{\mu\eta}$ and $\bar{\mu\theta}$ there is a gap from page 394, line 5 "| of the mind the" <note>original: "τοῦ νοῦ τὴν"</note> up to page 408, line 9 "to come to the | this however." <note>original: "παραγενέσθαι τῷ | τοῦτο δὲ"</note> The first gap covers a space of about six leaves, the second covers two full gatherings.

Learned scholia <term>scholia: ancient or medieval marginal explanatory notes</term> were added to the archetype during the Byzantine era $^{2}$) by a certain Christian. $^{3}$) Occasionally, interpretations of individual words seem to have crept into the main text (p. XXXIV).

---

1) cf. Heiberg in Kroll, *On the Chaldean Oracles* p. 2$^{1}$.
2) cf. scholia in **C** at p. 42, 16 and 97, 22.
3) *at* p. 122, 11ff: "You are most impious, but we, the race of Christians, are god-inspired and most pious." <note>original: "ὑμεῖς ἀσεβέστατοι, ἡμεῖς δὲ τὸ τῶν χριστιανῶν γένος ἔνθεον καὶ εὐσεβέστατον."</note>

# VI PREFACE

The silent "i" <note>original: "ι mutum"; referring to the iota subscript</note> was omitted by the ancient scribe. The letters **o** <note>omicron</note> and **ω** <note>omega</note> are very frequently confused with one another. Errors arising from so-called "itacism" <note>a common historical shift in Greek pronunciation where various vowels and diphthongs came to be pronounced like 'ee', leading to spelling mistakes</note> appear in no great number. The person who transcribed this copy of the book into minuscule letters <term>minuscules: a lowercase cursive style of Greek script</term> from an uncial archetype <term>uncial: an older style of script using large, rounded capital letters</term> often separated words incorrectly and applied false accents and breathing marks in many places; either he or a corrector following him is responsible for these faults, which you can still perceive today in the transcript.

In this book, I have primarily followed the spelling of the manuscript itself, writing **happening** <note>original: "γινόμενον" or "γιγνόμενον"</note>, **I know** <note>original: "γινώσκω" or "γιγνώσκω"</note>, **thus** <note>original: "οὕτω" or "οὕτως"</note> (before a consonant), **is and** <note>original: "ἐστὶ καὶ" or "ἐστὶν καὶ"</note>, **according to number** <note>original: "κατ' ἀριθμὸν" or "κατὰ ἀριθμόν"</note>, and **begotten** <note>original: "γενητὸς"</note> rather than **born** <note>original: "γεννητός"</note>; on the other hand, I have restored the movable **n** <note>original: "ν paragogicum"</note> before vowels where it was scattered and omitted. There is a singular instance of crasis <note>the blending of two words</note> on page 70, line 30: **therefore** <note>original: "οὖν Ↄ" i.e., "οὖν" for "ὁ ἐν"</note>, while the assimilation **in comedy** <note>original: "ἐγκωμῳδία" for "ἐν κωμῳδίᾳ"</note> (p. 73, 26) is less certain. The scribe divided the text into clauses.

A second hand corrected errors two or three times, as on page 238, line 15 and elsewhere. I shall demonstrate below (p. XII ff.) that a portion described from this very manuscript, or its twin, survives in the Riccardian Codex 24.

**Hermann Diels** and **Karl Kalbfleisch** collated the book in Paris in the year 1896; I examined doubtful passages in Munich during the winter of 1901/2.

<margin>**M**</margin> **MARCIANUS** Greek 195 (cf. Zanetti, p. 109) square, 29 x 20 cm, made of paper, from the late 14th and 15th centuries.

---

1) I include a few errors arising from the minuscule archetype: p. 196, 8 **in a straight line** (instead of **according to the divine**), p. 271, 2 **pastures** (instead of **shepherd**), p. 262, 15 **bringing together** (instead of **synagogue/assembly**), p. 374, 18 **it** (instead of **not the**), p. 381, 17 **to God** omitted after **good things**, p. 179, 29 **Heracles** [accusative] (instead of **of Heracles**), p. 454, 18 **the angular ones** [feminine] (instead of **the angular ones** [masculine]), p. 432, 19 **Dorkas** (instead of **the number ten**), p. 363, 21 **is or** (instead of **is and**), p. 139, 25 **of the sounds** (instead of **of the shapes**).

2) The following faults are explained by the uncial script <note>where letters like A, Δ, and Λ or O and Θ often looked similar</note>: p. 89, 27 **to be thick** (instead of **to do**), p. 120, 25 **of the cutting through** (instead of **uninterrupted**), p. 255, 2 **to us** (instead of **the one**), p. 257, 31 f. **to give to the bastard because** (instead of **to the bastard then because**), p. 302, 15 **how** (instead of **or as**), p. 345, 24 **we were all-persuaded** (instead of **we were all-disobedient**), p. 350, 1 **bright light** (instead of **nonsense**), p. 367, 29 **it is necessary** (instead of **always**), p. 369, 20 **concerning whom** (instead of **concerning God**), p. 432, 14 **those of this** (instead of **from it**, it should have been **those from it**).

It also identifies the primary manuscript source (Marcianus Graecus 195) and the scholars involved in its collation.</summary>

# CHAPTER ONE

The manuscript is composed of 396 leaves: 47 <term>quaternions</term> <note>gatherings of four folded sheets, making eight leaves</note> and 2 <term>quinions</term> <note>gatherings of five folded sheets, making ten leaves</note> (gathering ε, leaves 27—36, and gathering ια, leaves 77—86), which are numbered in the center of the lower margin of the first leaf of each gathering. The following gatherings were written by the first hand in a most elegant style on polished paper:

| Gathering | Leaves | Corresponding Text |
|----------|------------|----------------------------|
| β — δ | leaves 3—26 | p. 4, 12—81, 2 |
| ϛ — η | leaves 37—60 | p. 107, 4—186, 15 |
| ι — ιβ | leaves 69—94 | p. 211, 20—294, 13 |
| ιε — κγ | leaves 111—182 | p. 347, 17—171 C of the Basel edition |
| κε — κϑ | leaves 191—230 | p. 179 A — 216 D |

Thirty-five lines are written on each page of these gatherings. That these were once bound together in a single volume is shown by various signs, especially the water damage marks unique to these specific gatherings. Furthermore, I shall explain below (p. XI and following) that several gatherings never existed in this <term>codex</term> <note>a manuscript book</note>, nor in the copy from which it was transcribed, nor even in the original source of that copy.

The margins are adorned with very abundant and learned <term>scholia</term> <note>ancient or medieval marginal commentaries</note>, the greatest part of which agrees with the scholia of manuscripts C or B.

In the 15th century, either two scribes supplied the missing gatherings, or it was one and the same person. The first scribe (or the scribe in his first stage) wrote very carelessly on the last two leaves of gathering α (leaves 1 verso and 2 of the codex), the quinion ε, and leaves 61 and 62 recto of gathering ϑ. This work was performed with extreme negligence at the start—and not without reason, as the production of leaves 1, 2, and 27—36 (quinion ε) offered a specimen unworthy of even the slightest diligence. However, gatherings ϑ (in its entirety), ιγ, and ιδ flowed from a reasonably good source, very similar to manuscript N; I believe they come from the <term>Marcian 190</term> <note>A famous manuscript held in the Biblioteca Marciana in Venice</note> codex, concerning which see page XVII and following. To this same book perhaps belong gatherings κδ (leaves 183—190) and λ—μϑ (leaves 231—387 recto, followed by blank leaves 387 verso—390), where signs of the <term>vulgate recension</term> <note>the commonly accepted or "standard" version of a text</note> occur.

This <term>interpolated</term> <note>a text altered by the insertion of new material</note> codex presents five books of commentary; but the sheets of the "good" <term>recension</term> <note>a specific version of a text based on critical revision</note>, which I judge to be most precious due to the scribe's extreme diligence, end in—

The author identifies specific gatherings, notes water damage patterns, and links certain sections to the Marcian 190 manuscript and the vulgate tradition.</summary>

<keywords>codicology, quaternions, quinions, scholia, scribal hands, Marcian 190, manuscript transmission, Basel edition, paleography</keywords>

# VIII PREFACE

page 216 D of the Basel edition <note>original: "καθάπερ οἱ τελεστικοί φασιν"</note> *as the initiators of the mysteries say*.

The spelling of this book is generally the same as that of manuscript C: here and there it preserved the silent iota <note>The "iota subscript," a small mark under Greek vowels</note> (as on page 112, line 5 <note>original: "συνδιῴκει"</note> *governed along with*, page 117 in the Heraclitean scholium <note>A marginal explanatory note</note>, though incorrectly on page 144, line 31 <note>original: "μετάγειν"</note> *to transfer*). At the end of a line, however, both the addition of the movable "n" and the placement of an acute accent instead of a grave are due to the scribe's pen. The lemmas <note>The specific passages of text being commented upon</note> of Plato, for as many lines as they extend, are usually decorated with the mark ˃; passages from poets are distinguished by ˃ or χ or ͂; references to the scholia are rarely placed in the text with ÷ ÷, and in the margin with ※ or ◡; I found emendations and marginal additions joined to the text with the mark ∴

Errors arising from itacism <note>A common scribal error where different Greek vowels that sounded alike—like 'i' and 'ee'—were swapped</note> are not lacking. The book goes back to what seems to be an ancient model written in minuscule <note>Lower-case cursive script</note> letters<sup>1</sup>), since no certain traces of an uncial <note>Formal, rounded capital letters used in older manuscripts</note> specimen<sup>2</sup>) are found.

I believe that in the first quire <note>A gathered set of folded sheets of parchment or paper</note> of the archetype, besides the beginning of the commentaries, the book *On the Soul of the World and Nature* was inscribed, which is also prefixed to other manuscripts that imitate the model of the philosopher himself (cf. page 1, line 13 following).

The book belonged to Cardinal Bessarion<sup>3</sup>) <note>A 15th-century Byzantine scholar and Roman Catholic cardinal who saved many Greek manuscripts</note>, as the inscription on folio 1 verso shows:

->*Proclus the Platonist on the Timaeus. Location 53, the book of B(essarion) Car(dinal) of the Sabines, of Nicaea*<-

->*Proclus on the Timaeus, place 31 (<gloss>53</gloss>), the book of Bessarion, Cardinal of the Sabines.*<-

In the middle of folio 1 recto, a recent hand wrote *Twenty-four—24.*

I collated <note>Carefully compared the manuscript against other versions</note> these precious quires in Venice during the winter of 1899 to 1900 and in Munich in the summer of 1901.

---

1) β and μ confused with each other: p. 147, 13 <note>original: "ἐννεάμιοι"</note> *nine-fold*, p. 152, 29 *Iamblichus*, p. 174, 18 *Philemon* (so often, but p. 440, 29 *Philebus*), p. 213, 25 <note>original: "λοιβικῶν"</note> *of libations* (instead of *of plagues*). p. 178, 24 *nearer*.
2) cf. p. 178, 21 *greater [than] Libya* (instead of *greater [than] Libya* <note>The editor notes a confusion of letters typical of capital-letter scripts</note>), p. 356, 8 *it wishes to subsist there* (instead of *it subsists from there*), p. 400, 1 following *to beget* (instead of *to nod*). On the contrary, cf. the scholia p. 469, 18.
3) see Omont, *Journal of Libraries* 4 (1894) p. 137.

# CHAPTER ONE

->**P**<- PARISINVS National Library, Greek Collection 1840 [formerly ROYAL MEDICI 2213] (cf. H. Omont, *Inventory* II p. 151). Large format, 33.7 x 23.6 cm, made of paper, written in the 16th century, consisting of 158 leaves: nineteen <term>quaternions</term> <note>A gathering of four folded sheets, making eight leaves or sixteen pages.</note> and one <term>trinion</term> <note>A gathering of three folded sheets, making six leaves.</note> (δ̅). The numbers of these gatherings (α̅<sup>ov</sup> β̅<sup>ov</sup> γ̅<sup>ov</sup>), written by the scribe himself on the bottom margins, have for the most part perished due to the binding and trimming of the sheets. Since the handwriting is extremely small, especially at the beginning, the number of lines per page varies between 38 and 33. The text breaks off on leaf 156<sup>r</sup>, with the line left incomplete. A certain recent hand added in the lower margin: <insert>about ten leaves are missing</insert>; a different hand wrote on leaf 158<sup>v</sup>: <insert>Three books of Proclus the Platonic Successor on Plato's Timaeus, of which the third is incomplete and there is nothing more</insert> <note>original Greek: Πρόκλου Πλατωνικοῦ Διαδόχου τῶν εἰς τὸν Τίμαιον τοῦ Πλάτωνος τὰ τρία, ὧν τὸ τρίτον ἀτελὲς καὶ πλέον οὐδέν</note>; another even more recent hand noted a little below: <insert>twenty quires</insert> <note>original Greek: εἴκοσι τετράδια</note>.

Contained in this codex, which is of outstanding integrity, are the first three books up to page 228 A <note>referring to the standard Stephanus pagination used for Plato and his commentators</note> "and the whole" |. We might judge that the great gap from which this Paris manuscript suffers—from page 163, 29 "unitarily" | to page 186, 6 "surely and" |—arose from the loss of a single quaternion in the <term>archetype</term> <note>The original manuscript from which others were copied.</note>. This is especially likely since, at the end of the codex, two quaternions of the model appear to have been transposed. This is because from page 215 C "self-moving" | "for the straight [line]" through to the end of the codex, the text is transmitted in this order:
—Following the word "self-moving" (p. 215 C) are pages 221 E | "of another, one motion" up to page 228 A "and the whole" |;
—These words are followed by page 215 C "for the straight [line]" to page 221 E "their own motion, but they" (the final words of the codex).
This conclusion would hold, were it not for the Vatican Palatine codex 121; having consulted that, it must be established that two quaternions of the archetype perished, and four were transposed.

Although the <term>iota subscript</term> <note>A small 'i' written under a vowel in Greek, often omitted in later manuscripts.</note> is usually absent, the form "we shall push away" <note>original: ἀπαισόμεθα (i. ἀπασόμεθα)</note> is correctly preserved in **P** alone twice (p. 196, 29; 201, 7). Both the <term>lemmata</term> <note>The excerpts of Plato's text being commented upon.</note> of Plato and the passages of poets and writers are decorated with the **"** mark <note>a 'diplē' or quotation mark</note> along each line. In the margin, where one occasionally reads <margin>difficulty</margin> <note>original: ἀπο(ρια)</note> and <margin>solution</margin> <note>original: λύ(σις)</note>, there are rare numbers (1, 2, 3) relating to the arrangement of the context. In the same place, the scribe sometimes restored a truer reading, but <term>scholia</term> <note>Explanatory marginal comments or notes.</note> are entirely absent. How—

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the manuscript tradition for Proclus's commentary, focusing on the orthography of specific Greek terms and the physical characteristics of the Neapolitan manuscript.</meta>

# PREFACE

...even if words are divided according to the ancient custom: *he looked away* <note>original: ἀπέβλεπεν</note>, *it has been fastened* <note>original: ἐξῆπται</note>, *he attempted* <note>original: ἐπεχείρησεν</note>, *to the one who accused* <note>original: κατηγορήσαντι</note>, *establishing together* <note>original: συγκαθιδρύων</note>, *participation* <note>original: μετουσία</note>, *he presents* <note>original: παρίστησιν</note>, *he added* <note>original: προσέθηκε</note>, *to coexist* <note>original: συνυπάρχειν</note>, *existing* <note>original: ὑπάρχον</note>, *likewise* <note>original: ὡσαύτως</note>; the forms *I become* <note>original: γίνομαι</note> and *I know* <note>original: γινώσκω</note> and the Byzantine *begotten* <note>original: γεννητός</note> are written throughout in opposition to the **CM** manuscripts, from which it also departs by not eliding vowels at the ends of words. Errors of <term>itacism</term> <note>The tendency in later Greek to pronounce various vowels and diphthongs like the letter 'i'.</note> and foolish word separation are rare; one fault, as far as I have perceived, bears the traces of a <term>uncial archetype</term> <note>An original source text written in large, rounded capital letters used in early medieval manuscripts.</note>.$^1$)

The numbers due to a very recent hand which you read as $55$ (folio $19^r$), $206$ (folio $60^v$), $234$ (folio $70^v$), $314$ (folio $103^r$), $44\langle0\rangle$ (folio $156^r$) refer to the pages of the Paris manuscript 1838 (**D**). The Vatican Palatine 121 appears to be a close relative.

Examined at Paris in the year 1898, and at Munich in 1901.

**N** **NEAPOLITANUS BORBONICUS III D 28** (cf. Cyrillus II p. 419) of square shape (24.9 $\times$ 17.9 cm), made of <term>bombycine</term> <note>A type of silk-based or glazed cotton paper common in the Byzantine era.</note>, consisting of 164 leaves, i.e., 20 <term>quaternions</term> <note>Gatherings of four folded sheets, making eight leaves.</note>, to which four leaves (not a <term>binio</term> <note>A gathering of two sheets.</note>) have been attached. This codex, written in the year 1314, contains the first and second books of the commentary, which are preceded (folios $1^r$—$6^v$) by *Timaeus Locrus on the Soul of the World and Nature*. Not even this book is free from a large <term>lacuna</term> <note>A missing section or gap in the text.</note>; for the text is missing from page 250, 30 | *the one not knowing, but* <note>original: τὴν μὴ εἰδυῖαν ἀλλὰ</note> up to page 254, 13 | *the ever existing* <note>original: τὸ ἀεὶ ὄν</note> |: these same parts, which were previously omitted in folio 63 of the Marcian codex 190 (at folio $62^v$), were written separately from the rest by the first hand, joined to the preceding text by the mark $\daleth$ (cf. p. XIX).

On folio $164^r$ the following subscription is read:
> $\dagger$ *This present book was finished by the hand of me, Joannes Kartaris //// in the month of April, the 12th, in the year 6822 <note>The Byzantine year 6822 corresponds to 1314 AD.</note>.* $\div$
> . *From the testament of Antonius Seripandus$^2$)* *formerly of Janus Parrhasius$^3$)*.

The scribe who produced this copy of the book found the Proclian text increasingly decaying, as well as that of the common archetype...

# CHAPTER ONE

<note>original: "CAPVT PRIMVM"</note>

...errors by his own efforts <note>original: "suo Marte," a Latin idiom meaning by one's own skill or without help</note> sometimes not unsuccessfully, but more often too boldly attempted; for this reason, wherever I cite the readings of the codex <note>a "codex" is an ancient manuscript in book form</note>, I will not take note of mere trifles.
The Marcianus 190, the second hand of Marcianus 195, fragments of codex **R**, and the supplements of the Chisianus codex added by a different hand agree with the Borbonico in many readings: I will explain the details of each while describing the individual manuscripts.
I took specimens at Naples in the year 1900 and collated <note>compared the texts to identify variations</note> them at Munich in 1901, where either **C**, **M**, or **P** is missing.

<margin>**R**</margin> **FLORENTINE RICCARDIANUS 24** vellum, of the largest size, 31.5 x 22.5 cm. At my request, F. Boll attributed this book—which was discussed by Vitelli in *Italian Studies of Classical Philology* II (1894) p. 488—to the beginning of the 14th century. 22 gatherings of five sheets <term>quiniones: a gathering of five folded sheets of parchment</term> and two gatherings of four sheets <term>quaterniones: a gathering of four folded sheets</term> (the 4th, folios 31–38; the 24th, folios 229–236) provide 236 folios, the pages of which are filled with thirty-two lines. On the bottom of folio 236 verso, the Riccardianus fails in the middle of the third book of the commentary (page 191 E "concerning the bodies" <note>original Greek: περὶ τοῖς σώμασι</note>).
A single scribe produced the entire codex. However, the form of the text does not enjoy the same consistency throughout. For it descends from an exemplar compiled in a strange manner, which I have managed to reconstruct to some extent from the books still preserved.
We must begin from the "grandfather," so to speak, of codex **M**, which displayed the same gatherings as **M**:
->2–4, 6–8, 10–12, 15–23, 25–29<-
<margin>**e**</term> From the Riccardian recension <note>a "recension" is a critical revision of a text</note>, one can still define the beginning of the 2nd gathering, the 4th gathering, and the end of the 12th; the beginning and end of the 23rd gathering; and finally the beginning of the 25th gathering. Codex **R** ends in approximately the middle of the 26th gathering of codex **M**. Furthermore, there flowed into **R** from the same archetype <note>the original source from which later copies are derived</note> the complete 3rd, 6th, and 11th gatherings; almost half of the 6th gathering (page 123, 30 "the knowledge" <note>original: τὴν γνῶσιν</note> in **NR**: "knowledge" in **CMP$\varsigma$**; conversely page 124, 27 "carrying" <note>original: φερούσης</note> in **M**'s first hand and **R**: "guarding" <note>original: φρουρούσης</note> in **M**'s first hand, **CP$\varsigma$**); the greatest part of the 8th gathering (page 181, 27 "but that she herself is then the whole" <note>original: αὐτὴν δὲ ἄρα ὅλην εἶναι</note> in **MR** only, but page 183, 15 "power" <note>original: δύναμιν</note> omitted by **NR**); almost the whole...

XII
->PREFACE<-

gathering <term>quaternio: a "gathering" or "quire" of four folded sheets of parchment, making eight leaves</term> 10 <note>original: "ι̅"</note> (p. 213, 4 "more common" <note>original: "κοινοτέρων"</note> **NR**, but at p. 213, 9 the second "the" <note>original: "τὰς"</note> is omitted by **MR**). However, **R** shows no traces of gatherings 15 through 22: these had therefore perished by the time codex **R** was being written, which I have stated was at the beginning of the 14th century. It follows that codex **M** (from the end of the 14th century) and the parts of the Riccardianus just defined were not copied from the same source. On the contrary, long before codex **R**, a very carefully prepared copy of codex **M** flowed from those same parchment leaves while they were not yet torn or lost. The gatherings that had completely perished in the source of the Riccardianus, and the fragments substituted in **R**, were for the most part taken from a certain codex **N** which is similar, and very much like Marcianus 190: specifically, three pages of gathering 1, gathering 5, half of gathering 6, the end of gathering 7, gathering 9, and the beginning of gathering 10; finally, the entire context of the second book from p. 376/377 (cf. p. XIII). From p. 294, 13 | "nature" <note>original: "φύσιν"</note>, at the beginning of gathering 13 of codex **M** (**M²**), it is established that the Coislinianus or a twin of that same book was inserted to construct the Riccardianus—to bring forward the weightiest argument for this agreement—by a <term>lacuna: a gap or missing part in a manuscript</term> in codex **R**: for there fell out p. 319, 10 | "of the source-creators" <note>original: "τῶν πηγαίων δημιουργῶν"</note> up to p. 320, 13 "is the end" <note>original: "τέλος ἐστί"</note>, which <term>pericopa: a short extract or passage from a text</term> fills leaves 275 verso and 276 recto of codex **C**: evidently, in turning the parchment leaf 275 recto, the scribe skipped leaves 275 verso and 276 recto. Nevertheless, I would consider a twin of the Coislinianus not so much because of the corruptions and conjectures found in **R** ¹), as because of the words omitted in codex **R** where a "window" <note>a blank space left by a scribe when the source was illegible</note> was left ²); if these gaps seem not so much to be rejected as belonging to some copy older than the Riccardianus (cf. p. XLVII), but rather to the very source from which the author of the Riccardian <term>recension: a specific version of a text based on critical revision</term> drew, then that source was not **C**; for if that book were consulted, no one can see why words that are very easy to read there would have fallen out. Where the harmony between books **C** and **R** ends, it is not possible to accurately define:

# CHAPTER ONE

p. 376, 9 **appearing** <note>original Greek: φαινόμενον</note> **Manuscript C and the Revised Text**: **being carried** <note>original Greek: φερόμενον</note> **Manuscripts M, P, and the printed editions**, contrary to p. 376, 30.

<sig>Q</sig> **PARIS SUPPLEMENTARY** Greek manuscript 666 in the National Library (cf.
H. Omont, *Inventory III* p. 293) a square, silk-paper <note>term: bombycinus; paper made from silk or cotton fibers common in the 14th century</note> manuscript
from the mid-14th century, consisting of 349 leaves. The gatherings, of which there
are 44, are numbered in the middle or at the outer edge of the lower margin;
42 remain intact, however, since the 20th and 44th
consist of only seven leaves, the leaf of the last gathering,
which ought to have been 349, having been cut out. Furthermore, the number
121 appears twice (121, 121-bis): if all these things had not occurred,
we would have a total of 352 (8 $\times$ 44). Each page up
to leaf 143 verso contains 25 lines, while from leaf 144
onward the number of lines varies between 30 and 35. The codex is especially
difficult to read at the beginning, with the first leaves being fragmented
and ruined by dampness. The color of the ink is not always the same:
leaves 1–140 and 161 recto – 206 are written in a reddish ink; leaves
141–161 recto and 207–347 verso are in black. Even if from leaf 207
you seem to recognize a second hand, that same hand also wrote
several leaves and pages in the preceding sections.

The third, fourth, and fifth books are contained almost in their entirety, with only the
last part being torn near the end, similar to manuscript D.

The commentary ends on the upper part of leaf 347 verso. On the lower part of the page,
they have interpreted a seal as belonging to John Kantakouzenos <note>John VI Kantakouzenos, Byzantine Emperor and historian (reigned 1347–1354)</note> (the name of the owner
— the Emperor of the Byzantines?)

On leaf 349 verso a more recent hand added these words: **"Just as in the case of**
**sailors, whenever the pilot fails, he brings great**
**harm to those sailing with him, so also in cities..."** <note>A common Greek proverb regarding leadership</note>
**"...but (?) the sailor fails..."** and below, after another seal of
uncertain interpretation, **"Greatest Kings"** and a little further down
in red: **"L(?) | Of Proclus | Of Eleutherius | A gift."**

The silent iota <note>term: iota mutum; the iota subscript which was often omitted in medieval Greek</note> is mostly missing. A hand appears rarely in the margin.

# PREFACE

<note>original: "PRAEFATIO"</note>

added ancient and recent scholia <note>Scholia are marginal explanatory comments found in ancient manuscripts.</note>, and both the first and second hands marked the lemmas <note>A lemma is a word or phrase from the main text used as a heading for a commentary.</note> of Plato with the symbols ’ or ”.
I collated <note>To collate is to compare manuscripts word-for-word to identify variations.</note> this in Paris in the year 1898, and in Munich in 1901/2.

<margin>D</margin> **PARISINVS** National Library, Greek collection 1838 [MEDICEVS REG. 2093] (cf. H. Omont, *Inventory II* p. 151). It is of the largest format, 33.1 x 22.6 cm, consisting of 686 pages, written in the 16th century by John of Otranto; a specimen was provided by Omont, *Facsimiles of Greek manuscripts of the 15th and 16th centuries*, plate 28; cf. p. 12. It contains all the books (p. 1–684). From page 685 onwards, I published in the *Rhenish Museum* <note>original: "Musei Rhenani"</note> vol. 54 (1899) p. 196 the beginning of the *Melissa* <note>A "Melissa" or "Bee" was a common title for a florilegium or collection of excerpts.</note> of Proclus on Plato’s *Republic*.¹) This codex <note>A codex is an ancient manuscript in book form.</note> abounds with errors, gaps, and blank spaces <note>original: "fenestris," literally "windows," referring to holes or missing text in the source.</note>, especially at the beginning of the fifth book and toward the end. Frequently, the scribe clumsily disturbed the order of the leaves and the quires <note>A quire is a gathering of folded sheets of parchment or paper.</note> of the original model.
I have taken many samples. However, I shall call upon it for assistance after the failure of manuscripts **CMPN**, specifically from page 228 A | <insert>was composed in harmony</insert> <note>original Greek: "ἐναρμόνιον συνετέθη"</note>, so that manuscript **Q**, from which it is not far removed, does not lack support. This will be done in such a way, however, that we plainly omit the obvious sins attributable to the scribe's negligence, lest the image of the tradition be obscured.
A twin is the **Parisinus** National Library, Greek collection 1841. Very similar, although much less subject to errors and deformed by rash conjectures, are the **Chisianus** and **Escurialensis**: both preserved the fragment of the *Melissa*—certainly better. For this reason, I deeply regret that I could not use either manuscript in its entirety to restore the text: but I am persuaded that neither the **Chisianus** nor the **Escurialensis** is as valuable as **Q**, which I have fully exhausted. I will deal more extensively with this book in the preface to the second volume.
I consulted it in Paris in 1898, and partially collated it in Munich in 1901.

I shall list the manuscripts of the common recension <note>original: "uulgatae recensionis (ς)"</note> in the second chapter, except for the two upon which the earlier editions rely:

<meta>This page continues the cataloging of primary manuscript sources and early printed editions for the commentary on Plato's Timaeus. It focuses on Greek manuscripts held in Munich, Oxford, and Florence, as well as early Latin translations.</meta>

# CHAPTER ONE

<margin>A</margin> **The Munich Manuscript** (Munich State Library, Greek 382). A large-format paper manuscript from the 16th century. See Ignatius Hardt, *Catalogue of Greek Manuscripts in the Royal Library of Munich*, Volume IV, page 186. This codex <note>A "codex" is a bound book, as opposed to a scroll.</note> was the primary source used by **Christopher Schneider** of Breslau, who published his commentaries in 1847.

**Oxford, Corpus Christi College 98** (see *Catalogue of Manuscripts of Corpus Christi College*, page 34). A paper manuscript from the 16th century consisting of 477 leaves. On the front of the book is written: *This book was purchased from the heirs of William Grocyn <note>A famous English humanist and friend of Erasmus.</note> in the year of our Lord 1521, for Corpus Christi College, while John Claymond was President.* This is the "British Codex" from which <margin>b</margin> **Simon Grynaeus** first published these commentaries in Basel in 1534, alongside the works of Plato. Using this Basel edition, <margin>t</margin> **Thomas Taylor** translated the commentaries into the English language (London, 1820). A specimen of the text was kindly sent by the distinguished **Ingram Bywater**.

### EXCERPTS

<margin>L</margin> **The Laurentian Manuscript** (Florence, Laurentian Library, Greek shelf 28, codex 20; see Bandini, Volume II, page 39, VII). A square paper manuscript from the 14th century. On folio 266 recto, under the title *Excerpts from the Commentary of Proclus on the Timaeus* <note>original: "ἐκ τῆς εἰς Τίμαιον Πρόκλου ἐξηγήσεως"</note>, it presents excerpts beginning from page 192 DE: "And it must be known that the arithmetic mean is that upon which..." <note>original: "καὶ ἰστέον ὡς ἀριθμητικὴ μεσότης ἐστὶν ἐφ’ ἧς"</note> and ending on page 193 C: "...and they are reciprocal to one another" <note>original: "καὶ ἀντιστρέφουσιν ἀλλήλαις"</note> (folio 267 recto). I will explain the details in the preface to the second volume. I collated <note>Compared the texts for variations.</note> this in Florence in 1899.

*A translation of the passage from Plato's Timaeus regarding the generation of souls, with the explanation and digression of Proclus the Lycian.*
The inscription on folio 75 verso reads: *Niccolò Leonico Tomeo | con-*

# SUPPLEMENT TO THE FIRST CHAPTER
## OVERVIEW OF MANUSCRIPTS NOT USED IN THE RECENSION

**VATICAN PALATINE Greek 121** (cf. H. Stevenson, *Greek Manuscript Codices of the Vatican Palatine Library* p. 57) largest format (30.7 × 24.7 cm), paper from the 16th century, 194 leaves (consisting of 23 quaternions <note>A gathering of four folded sheets, making eight leaves</note>, one trinion <note>three sheets/six leaves</note> marked 14, and one binion <note>two sheets/four leaves</note> for leaves 191–194). It contains the first book of the commentary, marred by the same gap (leaf 187r) as Codex P. Since it also has a gap in a second place (leaf 192v) — for the section from page 191, line 20 <note>original: "ουργίας εἰκόνα"</note> up to page 203, line 7 <note>original: "Ἀθηναίους"</note> is missing — and this lost part is half the size of the former, one might guess that two quaternions of the archetype perished as well as one.

I would consider this book a sibling of Codex P (sharing the readings "heracleie" <note>original: "ἡρακλείη"</note> at p. 68, 20 and "anathemata" <note>original: "ἀναθήματα"</note> at p. 88, 16 with P), rather than a descendant (reading "through these" <note>original: "διὰ τούτων"</note> at p. 112, 28 against P).

I examined this in Rome in the year 1900.

**MARCIAN Greek 190** (cf. Zanetti p. 108) vellum, 31.4 × 22.5 cm, 14th century, 27 quinions <note>gatherings of five sheets, making ten leaves</note>, which correspond to 270 leaves of 37 lines each. Four blank leaves precede the first quinion; the last is followed by leaves 271r–281r (to which seven blank leaves are attached), presenting Proclus's *Commentary on the First Alcibiades*. This was transcribed less diligently in a slightly lighter ink by the same hand, which one may easily recognize from the scholia <note>marginal explanatory notes</note> added to the *Commentary on the Timaeus*. On the back of the first blank leaf, the following was written by a recent hand:

# PREFACE

*An epigram by Bessarion made for himself in Greek, translated into Latin by Theodore <note>1</note>:*
> *'Bessarion, while living, made this urn for his bones.*
> *His spirit itself has gone to the immortal God.' <note>2</note>*

*An epigram by Theodore made for the same Bessarion in Latin:*
> *'The Bishop of Nicaea, a prelate of the sacred Roman College <term>cardine: literally "hinge," referring to the College of Cardinals as the pivot of the Church</term>,*
> *Bessarion, a father of the highest piety,*
> *Whom the Black Sea <note>Pontus</note> brought forth and the land of Saturn <note>Italy</note> cherished,*
> *An exceptional glory to both Greeks and Italians,*
> *Is buried in this tomb. But he shines throughout the great world*
> *For his arts, eloquence, character, and genius.*
> *And he, whom the nations wept for when extinguished, remains blessed*
> *In his deeds, his ages, and his counsel.*
> *For him, the body has perished; but his fame shall never perish,*
> *So long as the swift heavens turn the bright stars.*
> *Give back your incense, O Greeks; give back your incense, O Latins, to the father.*
> *Whose virtue has made him enter the homes of the gods.'*

In the middle of the second blank leaf, the shelf-mark is read; on the back part of the fourth blank leaf, the most celebrated Cardinal wrote these words:

P,
T (that is, Place) 75 <note>The numbers are written over an erasure</note> Of Proclus the Lycian, Platonic philosopher and successor: Commentary on the Timaeus of Plato, in five books. It is indeed the possession of me, Bessarion, a Greek by birth, by dignity Bishop and Cardinal of Tusculum.

Place 75 <note>both numbers from an erasure</note> of Proclus the Platonist.

---

1) This would be Theodore Gaza, the humanist <note>original: "Theodoros Gazes"; see Krumbacher, History of Byzantine Literature, 2nd ed., p. 429 note, etc.</note>.
2) The epitaph of Bessarion still exists in Rome in the Church of the Holy Apostles <note>original: "Sanctis Apostolis"; see H. Vast, Cardinal Bessarion, p. 293 f.</note>: *Bessarion, Bishop of Tusculum, Cardinal of the Holy Roman Church, Patriarch of Constantinople, born and descended from noble Greece, placed this for himself while living, in the year of salvation 1466.*
->THAT IS: BESSARION,<-
->WHILE LIVING, COMPLETED THIS TOMB FOR HIS BODY,<-
->BUT HIS SPIRIT SHALL FLEE<-
->TOWARD THE IMMORTAL GOD.<-

# SUPPLEMENT TO THE FIRST CHAPTER

->The philosopher's exposition on Plato's Timaeus. This book is mine.<-
->*Bessarion, Cardinal Bishop of Tusculum.* <note>original: "b(essarionis) Car(dinalis) epi tusculani." Cardinal Bessarion (1403–1472) was a famous Greek scholar and prelate who contributed significantly to the revival of Greek letters in the West.</note> <sup>1)</sup><-

Folios 1 recto to 4 verso contain *Timaeus the Locrian, On the Soul of the World and Nature* <note>original Greek: Τιμαίω τῷ Λοκρῷ περὶ ψυχᾶς κόσμω καὶ φύσιος</note> (the title of this small book is painted in red); there follow five books of commentaries, of which the first two belong to the family of manuscript **N**. This is shown by various evidence, but especially by a gap <term>lacuna: a missing section of text</term> common to both books, which in this parchment has been filled by the insertion of a single leaf (folio 63). However, the numbering of the <term>quinio: a gathering of five sheets of paper or parchment</term> is not disrupted, because two leaves are marked with the number 61.

The text of the third, fourth, and fifth books closely approaches the standard printed version. On folio 266 verso, one encounters the same subscription <note>a concluding statement by a scribe</note> that ends the Munich Greek manuscript 382: *The commentary of Proclus on the Timaeus is found as far as the preceding points; whether he also explained the following parts is unclear.* <note>original Greek: ἕως τῶν προκειμένων εὕρηται τὰ εἰς τίμαιον τοῦ πρόκλου· εἰ δὲ καὶ τὰ ἑπόμενα ἐξηγήσατο ἄδηλον.</note>

The titles of the individual books are painted in red lead <note>minium</note>, and the initial letters are artistically gilded, except for the title of the third book (folio 182 verso), which was decorated only later.
Examined at Venice in the year 1899.

**PARISINVS** National Library, Greek 1841 [**FONTEBL.-REG. 2095**], paper, from the **16th** century (cf. Omont, *Inventory* II p. 151), is a twin of manuscript **D**.
I saw this in Paris in the year 1898.

**CHISIANVS R VIII 58** in the largest format, made of <term>bombycinus: silk-based paper or highly polished cotton paper</term>, **13th (?)** century, 233 leaves. The text, which had faded due to moisture and become full of gaps, seems to have been completed—if any faith is to be placed in the authority of the catalog—by the hand of Laurentius Portius, the scribe of Leo Allatius (folios 1–3 [= folios 4–6 repeated] and 231), and by another much older hand in "another elegant character" (folios 229, 230, 232, 233).

While it once contained five complete books, 32 leaves <note>original Greek: φύλλα λβ</note> have now perished before the fourth leaf, apparently four <term>quaterniones: gatherings of four folded sheets</term>. The ancient book now begins at page 130, line 23 | and <note>original Greek: καὶ</note>

# PREFACE

<note>original: "φθαρτά... τιμ"</note> perishable things, ends on folio 228 verso [with the words] "following the honor" (page 343 A).
Five leaves follow, written—as we have said—by a double hand, which contain both the end of the commentaries (folio 229 recto to the middle of folio 232 verso) and, after a blank space of about one line, the beginning of the *Proclian Bee* <note>The "Melissa" or "Bee" refers to a florilegium, a collection of excerpts or "choice flowers" of wisdom</note> (folios 232 verso to 233). A different hand added a portion of the supplements in the first and second books of the commentary, which I published in the *Rhenish Museum* <note>referring to the journal "Rheinisches Museum für Philologie"</note> volume 54 (1899) page 174 and following, both in the margin and in the text above the lines. However, the person who supplemented the Chisian manuscript used either the codex **N** itself or another very similar to it: never the Bourbon manuscript, which does not even contain all those additions itself; the augmented Chisian manuscript is more comprehensive.

Furthermore, this book stands on the side of codices **D** and the Escorial: the relationship between these books is easily recognized from the fragment of the *Bee*.

Ernest Lommatzsch inspected this in Rome in the year 1898, and I did so in 1900.

**ESCORIAL T III 2** (compare E. Miller, *Catalogue of the Greek manuscripts of the Library of the Escorial* page 133)
Square format. 402 paper leaves were written by various scribes in the 16th century. Although it once contained the entire commentary, the beginning is now lost up to page 116, line 13 [ending at the word] "destruction" |.
On folios 401 verso–402 verso is added the <term>Bee regarding the discourse of the Muses in the Republic</term> <note>original: "μέλισσα εἰς τὸν ἐν πολιτείᾳ λόγον τῶν μουσῶν"</note>.

At the foot of folio 402 verso, the following is read:
This book belongs to [erased name] and his friends <gloss>~of the beautiful</gloss>.

The related codices are **D** and the Chisian (compare page 14 and page 19 following).

Bruno Violet examined the book and collated the *Bee* for my sake in the year 1899.

**THE LUCCA MANUSCRIPT** <note>original: "LVCENSIS"</note> from the public library, 100 [1387] (cf. Festa, *Italian Studies of Classical Philology* V 1897 p. 223), paper in the largest format, written in the 15th/16th century, consisting of 489+42+10 leaves. It contains the five books of Proclus's commentary on the *Timaeus* with the subscription <note>a signature or note at the end of a manuscript</note> of the Munich codex. Attached by the same hand are written scholia <note>marginal explanatory notes</note> on the *Cratylus* (42 leaves), and by another hand **Josephus's treatise on the rule of reason** <note>original Greek: "Ἰωσήπου περὶ τοῦ αὐτοκράτορα εἶναι τὸν λογισμόν"; likely referring to 4 Maccabees, once attributed to Flavius Josephus</note> (10 leaves).
I saw this in Rome in the Royal Library in the year 1900, which codex Eugenius Boselli had kindly sent through the intervention of the Royal Ministry.

**THE MARCIAN MANUSCRIPT** <note>original: "MARCIANVS"</note> Greek 194 (cf. Zanetti p. 109) in octavo format (23.3 x 16 cm), paper from the 15th century (?), almost consumed by little worms. The leaves are not numbered.
There is also present, written by a different hand, an anonymous commentary on the first four books of Aristotle's *Physics*.
I identified this codex and the following one from photographs <note>original: "tabellas luce depictas"</note> provided by S. Morpurgo. Furthermore, I inspected both in Venice in the year 1899.

# REVIEW OF THE MANUSCRIPTS

There is no need to illustrate with examples the excellence of the tools upon which this edition relies, since innumerable passages of the commonly circulated text have been restored and many gaps, both small and large, have been filled. I demonstrated this four years ago in the *Rhenish Museum* <note>original: "Musei Rhenani"; a prominent German classical studies journal</note> volume 54, page 174 and following. It is not the purpose of this chapter to demonstrate this, nor to dwell on cases where individual books—whether **C**, **M**, or **P**—have suffered damage or been marred by errors. Rather, I have undertaken to write, insofar as it can be known from the manuscripts preserved to this day, a history of the text of Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher whose works were heavily studied and transcribed in the Middle Ages and Renaissance</note>. I believe I can best perform this task by arranging the most significant readings of individual books and classes into tables and briefly determining what may be concluded from each series.

Let it be permitted to set forth a survey of the best manuscripts, **C**, **M**, **P**, and **N**, showing how they are distributed throughout the individual pages of this volume:

| | **CMP(N)** | **CNP** | **CMN** | **MNP** | **NP** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| p. 1—4, 12 | — | — | — | — | **NP** |
| p. 4, 12—32, 2 | — | — | — | **MNP** | — |
| p. 32, 2—81, 2 | **CMP** | — | — | — | — |
| p. 81, 2—107, 4 | — | **CNP** | — | — | — |
| p. 107, 4—150, 19 | **CMP** | — | — | — | — |

In addition to the books <note>manuscripts</note> summoned to restore the <term>Proclian text</term> <note>The original writings of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, c. 412–485 AD</note>, I did not wish to omit the readings of the <term>vulgate recension</term> <note>original: "recensionis uulgatae (ς)"; the commonly accepted or "standard" version of a text circulating before modern critical editing</note> in these preliminary remarks, lest the final fates of the context be missing.

Let us begin from the **Coislinian** <note>The Codex Coislinianus, an important Greek manuscript</note> and see how the other <term>codices</term> <note>handwritten books</note> behave when this book alone exhibits the true reading.<sup>1)</sup> And first, indeed, all of them suffer from the same defects:

---

1) I preferred that the correct readings of the Coislinian drawn from the <term>Platonic lemmata</term> <note>original: "lemmasin Platonicis"; the short passages of Plato's text that Proclus quotes before his commentary</note> be absent from this small list, lest anyone should contend that those were not handed down by tradition, but were emended according to any random manuscript of **Plato**: p. 37, 5 *enemies* (instead of *men*), p. 59, 29 *to me in some way* (instead of *to someone*), p. 149, 9 *therefore* (omitted in others), p. 156, 15 *in the first* (instead of *firstly*), p. 187, 13 *but in later time, of earthquakes* (instead of *but of earthquakes*), p. 355, 17 *he established* (instead of *he brought together*), p. 409, 6 *having been finished* (instead of *having finished*).

| **p. 452, 22s <note>original Greek: δέδεικται τὸ αὐτοζῷον ὅτι ὄντως μόνον ἐστὶ μοναδικόν</note> it has been shown that the Living-Being-Itself is truly uniquely one** | **<note>original Greek: ἐν δέδεικται τὸ αὐτοζῷον καὶ ὄντως (ὄντα ς) μόνον ὡς ἔστι μοναδικόν</note> in [which] it has been shown the Living-Being-Itself and truly (being, in manuscript ς) alone as it is uniquely one** |

The defects with which we have seen all the manuscripts labor, except for **C**, are so serious that they must certainly have flowed from the same source. And yet, not all those books always fell into the same errors: many passages in **MP** (or **MN**, **NP**) are, so to speak, lightly wounded, whereas in the **ς** family <note>The symbol ς often refers to a later group of manuscripts or a specific printed tradition</note> they are read as either plainly corrupt or disguised with a false luster of truth.

The middle column, which provides the <term>scripturas</term> <note>readings or written variants</note> of the <term>codicum</term> <note>manuscripts</note> **MP (MN, NP)**, indicates a stage in which it is impossible not to think that many <term>lectiones</term> <note>readings</note> of the <term>recensionis uulgatae</term> <note>the "vulgate" or common edition</note> occasionally halted.<sup>1)</sup> I say "many readings," for the same would apply to the entire context of ς, if—leaving aside **N** for now—the <term>archetypus</term> <note>the original lost manuscript from which others are copied</note> from which ς descends were to be reconstructed from the agreement of manuscripts **M** and **P**.

1) However, the truth of the <term>stemmatis</term> <note>the "family tree" of manuscripts</note> is not shaken by arranging the readings in this way: **C : M : P ς** (p. 281, 18 *for* [first instance]: *for* with *but* written above: *but*) and **C : P : M ς** (p. 58, 30 *universal and set apart*: *universally and set apart*: *universally and separately*; p. 218, 17 *he says*: *he says, he claims*: *to say, he claims*; p. 266, 11 *offspring*: *offspring* with *eng* written above: *progeny*; p. 284, 15 *not such a thing of the begotten*: *such a thing of the begotten*: *nothing such of the begotten*): either we are dealing with various ancient readings, or **M** as well as ς attempted to heal the <term>mendis</term> <note>errors or defects in the text</note> with less than total success.

But from the comparison of readings <note>original: "lectionum conspectu"; a list showing how different manuscripts record the same passage</note>, which I shall now add, it will be easily seen that the errors into which manuscripts **MP** fell—as opposed to the group **ς**—are too serious to have all been removed from **ς** by mere guesswork <note>original: "coniectura"; an editor's educated guess to fix a broken text</note>, though no one will deny this happened in one place or another (see page XXXII and following). It appears, therefore, that while **C**, **M**, and **P** are occasionally deformed by their own specific errors, the reading of the common archetype <note>The 'archetype' is the lost original manuscript from which all surviving copies descend</note> will usually emerge from the agreement of the parchment copies, both **CM** and **CP**. But since not only does **C** have many things at which we might take offense, but even codex **M**—certainly a very good one—stands today as a distinguished witness of a mutilated original, we cannot do without **P**, a most upright book related to **M**, throughout the entire commentaries. # PREFACE

The context of **MP** <note>Manuscript sigla referring to specific codices</note> would stand corrupted if it relied solely on the faith of codices **NDς**: page 308, lines 10 and following, the words "the fathers — kings" <note>original Greek: οἱ πατέρες — βασιλεῖς</note> fell out in **CPA** due to *homoeoteleuton* <term>homoeoteleuton: a scribal error where the eye skips from one word to another with a similar ending</term>, but were preserved by **NDb**. On page 303, lines 12 and following, the word "to find" <note>original Greek: εὑρεῖν</note> perished in **C** after "another" <note>original Greek: ἕτερον</note>, while the entire sentence is missing in **NP**. The situation is similar regarding the word "essentialists" <note>original Greek: οὐσιῶται</note> (page 364, line 19) which was omitted in **CP**, while **M** suffers from a larger gap; compare page 116, line 4 "habit" <note>original Greek: ἕξιν</note>, page 364, line 29 "needy" <note>original Greek: ἐνδεῆ</note>, and page 375, line 2 "indeed" <note>original Greek: μὲν</note>: these three readings are correctly transmitted only in **NDς**.

Now indeed, let us consider what is concluded from the relationship between the books which we have just established: wherever the Coislinian reading <note>Referring to the Fonds Coislin collection in Paris</note> seems preferable with the support of **M** (or **P** or **N**) against **P** (or **M** — I might omit **N** again —) and **ς**, it must concern the most trivial errors, which were clearly committed twice. And certainly, to one examining the various writings of this volume, the readings that occurred in the patterns **CM**: **Pς**, **CP**: **Mς**, **CN**: **Mς**, **CN**: **Pς**, were almost all caused by *homoeoteleuton*, spelling faults, or *itacism* <term>itacism: a pronunciation shift in Greek where different vowels came to be pronounced like 'ee', leading to spelling errors</term>, such as the confusion between "before" and "towards" <note>original Greek: πρὸ and πρὸς</note> or the exchange of "o" and "long o" <note>original Greek: ω and ο</note>. However, I would not conceal those differences which seemed more serious, collected with the greatest diligence; if you examine these more closely against the context itself, you will determine with me that they either crept in by chance in both **P** (or **M**) and **ς**, or that they display traces of a very ancient variety of writing, such as on page 122, line 20 where "excessively" <note>original Greek: ἄγαν</note> is repeated, page 441, line 26 "later" versus "second" <note>original Greek: ὕστερον: δεύτερον</note>, page 445, line 13 "scattered" versus "dispersed" <note>original Greek: σκεδαννύμενα: σκιδνάμενα</note>, page 203, line 16 where "containing" <note>original Greek: χωροῦσαν</note> is omitted, and others.¹)

| p. 341, 2ss **of those saying — of things** <note>original Greek: λεγόντων — πραγμάτων</note> | omitted | **the exact truth, but others the probable** <note>original Greek: τὸ ἀκριβὲς τοὺς δὲ τὸ εἰκὸς</note> |

No one will now doubt that, whatever the other fates of the Neapolitan manuscript were, the archetype <term>archetypus; the lost original from which a group of manuscripts is copied</term> of the common revision is found from the agreement of manuscripts **MP** and **N** (just as with **MP** and **Q** or **MP** and **D**). If this is so, whatever good readings are owed to **CNς**, those necessarily remained unharmed through all the centuries. If, however, at times **MPN** seem to hang together more closely than **Cς**, the common version has been made to resemble manuscript **C** by conjecture (see page XXVIII).

---

1) It is possible, therefore, that **CN** show the true reading, while the **MP** revision is corrupted in one way and the common version in another: p. 419, 24 **in which also the** <note>original: ἐν ᾧ καὶ τὸ</note> **CN**: **above also the** <note>original: ἄνω καὶ τὸ (the omitted in P)</note> **MP**: **where also the** <note>original: οὗ καὶ τὸ</note> **ς**; p. 456, 18 **as we said then**: **nor (if P) then should we say**: **as we said then**; p. 457, 25 **having become**: **it became as**: **a thing having become** **ς**; p. 370, 24 **a casting of the mind** <note>original: νοῦ βολὴν</note> **CN**: **counsel of the mind** <note>original: νοῦ βουλὴν</note> **MPς** you might easily interpret. Nevertheless, p. 434, 3s is not without all difficulty: **very beautiful god** <note>original: περικαλλὴς θεὸς</note> **CN** perhaps correctly: **son of the very beautiful ether** <note>original: περικαλλέος αἰθέρος υἱὸς</note> **Pς**: **to be surrounded by summer son** <note>original: περιβαλλέσθαι θέρος υἱὸς</note> **M**: could an ancient variant reading <term>uaria lectio</term> lie beneath this?

# CHAPTER TWO XXXIII

From this it follows that the diagrams Cς : NP and Cς : MN must be interpreted in such a way that the true readings of the class ς <note>The Greek letter sigma (ς) is used here as a technical symbol (siglum) to represent a specific group or family of manuscripts.</note> should be attributed to correction rather than to tradition. Indeed, just as one would hardly believe that all these same corruptions arose purely by chance in **P** (or **M**) and **N**, so too one could hardly heal those obvious errors in any other way than was done in ς, which sagaciously restored the correct reading of codex **C** by its own ingenuity.

We have learned that the text of the commentaries, having worsened more and more through the individual centuries, has not reached us by a straight path. Just as all the codices we know of descended from one and the same <term>archetype</term> <note>The original lost manuscript from which all surviving copies are derived.</note>, so **MPNς** flowed from a common source different from book **C**; thereafter both **MP** as well as **M** and **P** underwent their own fates, while **Nς** traveled together for a short time. However, with **N** deviating, and the tradition persisting again chiefly in **Q** and **D** (Chisian and Escorial manuscripts), ς represents the lowest level of the tradition.

Where Codex **C** has perished or is corrupt, the instruments for restoring the text are **MP(N)ς** or **MNς** or **NPς**: individual—

->PROCLUS edited by Diehl. Vol. I. c<-

# XXXIV PREFACE

It would be tedious to bring forward examples of all the classes. The matter at hand is rather to determine where manuscript **C** has fallen away when the other books do not agree among themselves, and then which book or books have retained the truth. However, it is not permitted to separate one question from the other. If the Coislinianus and the ϛ <note>The Greek letter 'stigma', used here to denote a specific group or family of manuscripts</note> suffer from their own specific faults against **MP**, it contains nothing strange:^1) we usually draw the truth from **MP**. But if **C** acts in concert with another certain codex or other books, you will be persuaded with me that two paths of explanation lie open: either most books along with **C** have strayed very close to the truth, or individual books **M** or **P** (or **N**) have found the correct reading through easy emendation <note>The process of correcting a text through logical deduction rather than direct evidence</note>.

I shall take my beginning from those places where **C** and ϛ have gone astray into a bypass against **MP** (**MN**, **NP**). I plainly omit the lighter instances. As for those that remain, no one would value them so highly as to dare to contend that a closer familiarity exists between ϛ and **C**: for I have no doubt that *limit* <note>original: "πέρας"</note> instead of *boundary* <note>original: "πεῖραρ"</note> (p. 385, 30), and *it happened* <note>original: "ἐγένετο"</note> instead of *it became* <note>original: "γένετο"</note> (p. 313, 20) were ancient scholia <note>marginal comments or explanations written by ancient teachers or scribes</note> badly received into the text, just as on p. 140, 14 *they deified* <note>original: "ἐθείωσαν"</note> in **MP**ϛ: *they lived* <note>original: "ἐβίωσαν"</note> in **C** (<margin>margin **Δ**</margin>), and p. 435, 4 *such a kind* <note>original: "τοιοῦτον"</note> instead of *such* <note>original: "τοῖον"</note>. The rest require no explanation.

Sometimes the reading of Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher whose commentaries are vital for reconstructing ancient texts.</note> is recognized from only a single manuscript, **M** or **P**: in some of these examples, you might rightly suspect a later correction, while in other cases, the original readings have remained intact by a stroke of good fortune; you may easily discern which is more likely in each individual instance. As for the Neapolitan manuscript, since it has been subjected more than all others to what we call a <term>philological recension</term> <note>A systematic critical revision of a text by a scholar-editor.</note>, I have so far rejected its true readings alongside its corrections of the common text, so as not to confuse likely traditions with certain conjectures.

Just as one must judge the true readings of the books **M** or **P**, so must one judge the patterns of **Mς : CP** or **CN**, and **Pς : CM** or **CN**; I will immediately subjoin the arguments for these, whatever seemed worthy of note. 1) It truly makes no difference whether those correct readings, located very close to corrupt ones, have reached us intact from antiquity or have been restored by a very slight change (especially since in more than one place both are true, so that the reading of a good manuscript rests on tradition, while the common revision rests on conjecture), because some errors that seem serious (p. 161, 26 "the interval" <term>omitted</term>; p. 141, 23 "place" instead of "time"; p. 210, 30 "having favored" instead of "having completely favored"; p. 445, 30 f. "still... it falls upon" instead of "it is... to fall upon") even today show traces of a variety of writing. 2) In this paragraph also, you will learn below (p. XLIV f.) that I have rightly passed over the patterns **Nς : CP** and **Nς : CM**.

| | **Mς** | **CP** |
|:---|:---|:---|
| p. 161, 26 f. | this interval (the word "interval" is in **M**) | this |

---

1) Furthermore, **M** or **P** exhibit the truth even when compared with individual common manuscripts: p. 212, 30 "connective" **MA**, p. 457, 17 "uniqueness" **Mb**, p. 51, 5 "if the" **PA**, p. 103, 25 "the younger" **Pb**, p. 314, 7 "but in" **Pb**.
2) On the other hand, one may think less of conjectures wherever **C** is corrupted in one way and **P** (or **M**) in another: **Mς : C : P** (p. 41, 8 "nourishment" [acc.] : "nourishment" [nom.] : "of nourishment"), **Mς : P : C** (p. 62, 25 "better then instead of" : "better would be instead of" : "better instead of"; p. 355, 6 "and some things" : "and the things" : "but the things"), **Mς : P : C** (p. 439, 16 "of his whole self" : "his whole self" : "wholly of him"), **Pς : M : C** (p. 428, 16 "the very first" : "someone the first" : "who the"), **Pς : C : N** (p. 104, 18 "of these" [agreeing with Plato] : "this" : "of this").

The image of the tradition of the text of Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher whose commentaries are being edited here</note> which has appeared to us in comparing those readings where **C**—either alone or with other books—shows the true version, is not at all obscured by the fact that either **C** alone was corrupted or several codices (or just one book along with **C**) departed from the truth. Therefore, I believe I have rightly omitted the "common reading" (ς) <note>The *vulgata* or commonly accepted version before this critical edition</note> from the <term>apparatus criticus</term> <note>The notes at the bottom of a page showing variant readings from different manuscripts</note> which I placed below the text. I did this so that the annotations would not grow beyond measure by including the errors, gaps, and trifles of the common version. Nevertheless, you might object that I have used three sources even in those parts of the commentary where **CMP** exist together: ς and—with **CMPς** agreeing among themselves—**N**, and very rarely **R**.

And first, indeed, we have called upon ς for help wherever it displayed or seemed to display the correct reading.<sup>1</sup>) Although you may easily gather examples from the annotations, nevertheless the lists—

1) If anyone should ask why I did not instead adopt **N** or **D** (and its relatives) in all those places, I will answer that **N**, while making many emendations in agreement with ς, has been too much deformed by other changes which are not emendations, and even more so—

I shall gather these into a few small groups so that the cleverness of the corrector may be more clearly exposed. However, whatever we have drawn from that <term>recension</term> <note>original: "recensione"; a specific version of a text resulting from a systematic revision</note> or even from individual members of the <term>vulgate class</term> <note>original: "classis uulgatae"; the common or standard version of the text circulating at the time</note> cannot be of the highest antiquity. It is absolutely necessary to conclude that all those readings can be explained by <term>conjectures</term> <note>original: "coniecturis"; educated guesses made by scholars to fix broken text</note>—the kind that we ourselves are able to devise—except for a few passages which we excepted above (page XXIX and following).

# PREFACE

The text, which seemed to be confirmed by countless readings of the manuscripts **CMP (N) ς** <note>These letters refer to specific historical copies of the text: C, M, and P are the primary group, N is the Naples manuscript, and ς (sigma) represents the later printed editions or "received" text.</note>, was causing some doubt. Therefore, if the writing in **ς** was further removed from the tradition according to the consensus of the books **CMP** than could be resolved by a simple correction, I undertook to find something more truthful. Success did not fail my attempts. In the Naples manuscript—which was most shrewdly corrected in many places—I found quite a few approved conjectures; other errors, handed down by **CMPN** but neglected by **ς**, I believe I have now removed.

In the second place, wherever we have hesitated in restoring the text while the manuscripts **CMPς** were in agreement with one another, we have examined the Neapolitan manuscript <note>The "Codex Neapolitanus," a specific historical book held in Naples.</note>, upon whose fate it is necessary for us to dwell for a little while. And first, indeed, it corrected several faults more successfully than **ς** did, as we saw above, although it occasionally yields to it.¹) Both recensions <note>A "recension" is a specific version of a text resulting from a scholar's revision.</note> healed many passages in the same way, just as many errors were common to both; for which reason we have gathered that **N** and **ς** are more closely connected to each other.

Now indeed, since **N**, as far as the reliability of the transmitted text is concerned, is by no means of the greatest importance, in those parts of the commentary where **N**—acting in the place of a secondary manuscript—alone offered the correct reading, you might attribute this to emendation <note>intentional correction by a scribe</note> rather than to a memory that remained uncorrupted in this single book. For this reason, while I illustrated the superiority of manuscripts **M** and **P** above (p. XXXV ff.), I believe I rightly omitted **N**: you will perceive many things that were conjectured at first glance.

On the other hand, wherever **CM** or **CP** have not suffered the same "disaster" <note>The author uses "cladem" (disaster/defeat) to refer to a shared scribal error</note>, provided that one is not clearly corrupted from the other (as we have seen **C** and **P** are depraved versions of the writing in **M**), nothing prevents you from drawing the reading of the <term>archetype</term> <note>The original lost manuscript from which others descend</note> from **N$\zeta$**, while the other codex preserves traces of the correct spelling.

# PREFACE

| Page and Line | Reading A | Reading B | Reading C |

| p. 321, 9 <note>original Greek: λέγωμεν</note> let us say | <note>original: λέγομεν</note> we say | <note>original: λέγοιμεν</note> we might say |

| p. 325, 7 <note>original: ἦν νοητῶς</note> was intelligibly | <note>original: νοητῶς</note> intelligibly | <note>original: ἡ νοητῶς</note> the [one] intelligibly |

In addition to manuscripts **N** and **ς**, I have examined a third, **R**, in doubtful passages. Although it is very closely related to **M** and **N**, it has only very rarely corrected the errors found in all the other books. It does, however, correctly provide:
p. 20, 15 "the" <note>neuter</note> (instead of "the" <note>masculine</note>),
p. 88, 16 "offerings" <note>original: ἀναρρύματα</note> (where others have "drawings up" or "sacrifices"),
p. 94, 1 "most justly" from Plato (where others have "most just things"),
p. 184, 28 "having set before" (where others have "having set before or having added and"),
p. 197, 10 "second" <note>masculine/neuter</note> (instead of "second" <note>feminine</note>),
p. 436, 1 where the manuscripts **MP** have "so that always as," **N** changed it to "so that perfectly," but **R** wrote "so that perfectly."

But it is necessary for us to dwell on **R** not so much because of the good readings found here and there, but because of the kinship by which it is joined with the books **M¹**, **N**, and **M²**.
In those parts of the commentaries where **R** aligns with **M¹**, such a great agreement shines forth between both books—not only in the best readings of codex **M** (see p. XXXV) and various more significant writings, but even in the slightest differences of orthography¹) (though otherwise the faithfulness to the original was by no means most religiously preserved by **R**²))—that unless <term>palaeography</term> <note>the study of ancient handwriting</note> utterly forbade it, we would believe that **R** was copied directly from book **M**. In those...

---

1) p. 7, 24 and 8, 14 "Pythagorean" in **MR**, p. 14, 24 "of those having met," p. 22, 8 "attending the school": "attending the school," p. 31, 10 "to talk nonsense," p. 174, 18 and others "Philemus," p. 176, 30 "infinite things of the best" (instead of "infinite things from the indefinite"), p. 221, 12 "to the council" (instead of "by will"). In the <term>scholia</term> <note>ancient marginal comments</note> of **R** p. 464, 9, since the scribe did not understand the abbreviations depicted in codex **M**, he omitted the words entirely. The marginal notes of **M** also reappear in **R**, such as p. 255, 29 "it is written 'approach'"; more often he either completely neglected them (p. 124, 27 "bearing" in the text of **MR**: "guarding" in the margin of **M**; p. 80, 13 "or the paradox — O Socrates" in the margin of **M**: omitted in **R**; p. 245, 19 "of the — 20 souls" in the margin of **M**: omitted in **R**) or interpreted them incorrectly (such as the scholia of **M** being taken into the main text: p. 20, 6 "to the Eleatic," p. 24, 2 "Timaeus").
2) For example p. 8, 30 "is such" in **R** (instead of "is of such a sort"), p. 11, 21 "about the gods" (instead of "about the goddess"), p. 18, 26 "altogether" (instead of "primarily"), p. 19, 9 "but the [thing]" (instead of "but by the [way]"), p. 20, 10 "of social gathering" (instead of "of death through disease"), p. 21, 29 "properly" (instead of "proportionately"), p. 78, 8 "he has": "it happened."

# CHAPTER TWO

However, in those places where **R** has strayed through a certain negligence, it appears that manuscript **D** — whatever the other fates of that book were — cannot be separated at the start from manuscript **N** (page 5, line 15 <note>original Greek: τῆς αὐτοῦ</note> "of him" in **ND**: "of her" <note>original: τῆς αὐτῆς</note> [in others]; p. 6, 11 "he says": "he leaves off"; p. 11, 6 "customs": "in the custom"; p. 12, 22 "axle": "dry"; p. 23, 24 "him": "her"; p. 27, 18 "in them": "in him") nor indeed can it be separated from book **R**, which it supports not only in those false readings (see page 46, note 2). Furthermore, unique readings from manuscript **P** occur in **D**: p. 17, 11 "they were begetting" <note>misspelled as ἐγείνων for ἐγέννων</note>, p. 42, 21 "brain" (instead of "head").

Furthermore, I shall discuss in a few words the necessity by which **R**, **N**, and **M$^2$** <note>M$^2$ refers to the second hand or corrector of manuscript M</note> are linked.$^1$) **R** is not as narrowly joined with **N** as it is with **M$^1$**, even if in the sections defined above the unique writings of manuscript **N**, of which I have cited many, nearly all reappear in **R**. However, we demonstrated on page 46 that in the Riccardian manuscript, some readings were restored by emendation against the consensus of all other books. Moreover, the corrector rarely finds — for there is no evidence to suggest he consulted another book — what is read in other manuscripts (such as p. 194, 17 "neither" in C, the printed editions, and R: "not yet" in NP). In many ways **R** departs from **N**, such that the Riccardian reading is clearly corrupted from the writing of **N** itself; **M$^2$**, as I shall immediately add, almost always sides with **R**:

| (**M$^2$**) **R** | **N** | The True Reading <note>Latin: uerum</note> |
|-------------------|-------|-------|
| p. 82, 20 for nothing | nothing | for it differs nothing |
| p. 93, 20 ff. touching upon | sharpening | rejoicing in |
| p. 112, 29 ff. to be covered by | [omitted] | sudden disturbances and falls of the air to happen |
| p. 202, 3 ff. but he says that of Timaeus | for the sake of this difficulty | for the sake of this difficulty but of another — 5 genuinely |
| p. 203, 1 which he even now imitates | who even now imitates | who even imitates the Mind <note>Greek: νοῦν (Nous)</note> |

---

1) Especially from the beginning of the commentary to p. 4, 12 (the start of manuscript **M$^1$**), **NR(D)M$^2$** cannot be separated at all: p. 1, 7 "distinct" ("vivid" in P and the printed editions), p. 2, 2 "being divided" ("having been divided"), p. 2, 8 "placing under" ("being placed under").
