It would be a long task to bring forward all the faults and foolish conjectures ¹) by which manuscript **R** departs from **N**. I should consider it sufficient to have mentioned that almost all the bad readings of codex **R**—specifically in those parts of the commentary where **R** follows the **N** recension <note>A "recension" is a specific version of a text based on critical revision</note>—reappear in **M²** ²), but in such a way that **R** seems to be slightly further from the truth than **M²**: for example, on p. 82, 20 "for nothing": "nothing" in **N** and **M²**; p. 197, 26 "having" in **R** (and p. 198, 18 "to have"): "leading" in **C, N, P, ς,** and **M²** ("to lead"); p. 203, 14 "the gods as laws" in **R**: "the law as the gods" in **M²**: "law as gods" in **C, N, P, ς**.

From p. 294, 13 up to p. 347, 17, where **R** originated from **C** itself or from a twin manuscript, **N** and **M²** must be compared against the other books. The unique readings of the Neapolitan manuscript (such as p. 316, 18 "the poet and father" inserted after "but the decad," p. 324, 29 "first-born," p. 325, 15 "he was at a loss," p. 331, 22 "very first," p. 340, 2 f. "not... of soulless things," p. 345, 24 "persuasive") are not lacking in **M²**. However, even here **M²** cannot have descended from the Borbonico <note>The "Codex Borbonicus," another name for the Neapolitan manuscript N</note>, as shown especially by p. 331, 14 f. "but rather by the stronger," which is omitted in **N**, while **M²** preserved "but by the stronger," which in my opinion was provided by the Marcianus 190 manuscript.

From the most excellent codices <note>Handwritten manuscript books</note> upon which the text rests, this family tree has emerged:

---

1) As on p. 99, 25 "whenever" (instead of "even if," it should have been "if"), p. 104, 1 "to say" (from Plato) instead of "he said," p. 396, 16 "of simple things" from "from the" (correctly in **ς** "of quality-less things").

2) p. 190, 3 "disordered" in **R** and **M²** ("well-ordered" in **N**), p. 191, 8 "filling up" ("sailing back"), p. 193, 14 "to find out by" ("to find upon"), p. 193, 17 "as"—18 "having taken up" omitted in **R** and **M²**, on p. 194 and 198 **R** and **M²** contracted the lemmas <note>The theme-word or passage from an author being commented upon</note> of Plato, p. 202, 20 "looking away" ("beholding"), p. 207, 20 "all-wondrous" ("again wondrous"), p. 209, 2 "salvation" ("history") and many others.

# CHAPTER TWO

<image-desc>A family tree of manuscripts (stemma codicum) showing the descent of copies. At the top is the "uncial archetype" <note>a master copy written in large, rounded capital letters</note>, which leads down to the "minuscule archetype" <note>a master copy written in smaller, cursive script</note>. From this minuscule source, several branches emerge: one leads to manuscript C (B); another splits into M (R) and P; a third leads to N (R). Further down, the lineage forks into Q and D, finally ending at manuscript S. Dotted lines indicate the connections between these various historical copies.</image-desc>

Regarding the relationship that exists between the excerpts of Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a major Neoplatonist philosopher whose commentaries on Plato are vital for understanding ancient thought</note> found in the scholia <note>marginal notes or comments made by ancient or medieval scholars</note> of Plato and the Proclian text handed down in the manuscripts, the following must be maintained: that the reading of the superior books is certainly preserved by the Platonic scholia. One might rightly wonder, therefore, why no one has yet undertaken to correct those parts of the commentary using the scholia, even though many have seen that not only the scholia read under the name of "Proclus" <note>original: Πρόκλου</note> actually trace back to him (see Mettauer, *On the Sources of the Scholia of Plato*, Zurich, 1880, p. 8 ff.). I shall cite only one reading, as it confirms the judgment we have made regarding the preservation of the commentary's text: on page 188, line 6, the manuscript **N** alone (and its relatives **BM²**) shows the true writing *histan* <note>original: ἱστὰν</note>, while **P** and the Platonic scholia show *hista* <note>original: ἱστᾶ</note>; **C** shows *hista* <note>original: ἱστα</note>; and the printed editions show *histētai* <note>original: ἱστηται</note>. Since the reading handed down from antiquity is *hista*, we correctly determined on page XLIV that *histan* must be due to a later conjecture. Furthermore, the scholiast of Plato [discusses] the relationship of Solon and both men named Critias...

<summary>This page analyzes the textual genealogy of Proclus's commentaries, arguing that marginal notes in Plato's manuscripts are essential for reconstructing the original text. It includes a diagram showing the descent of various manuscript families from ancient archetypes.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Plato, scholia, manuscript tradition, stemma codicum, textual criticism, Solon, Critias</keywords>

# PREFACE

It is obvious to everyone that [the author] transcribed (regarding *Timaeus* 20 E) from the lineage of manuscript **C** (referring to page 82, line 5 and following): this is shown both by the order of the names and especially by the omission of the name *Adeimantus* in manuscripts **R** and **ς** <note>The symbol ς often refers to a specific family of late manuscripts or a printed edition used as a reference</note>.

Besides the commentator on Plato’s *Timaeus*, a certain anonymous Christian author—who wrote the dialogue *Hermippus, or On Astrology* (edited by Kroll and Viereck, Leipzig 1895)—copied a few passages from these commentaries. I have marked the notable passages in the critical apparatus <note>The 'apparatus criticus' is the set of notes at the bottom of a page documenting variations in different manuscripts</note> and included the rest in the supplements. However, since that author very rarely cited Proclus word-for-word, nothing certain can be determined regarding the specific copy he used: on page 70, line 5, he omitted the word "this" (*tautēs*) along with manuscript **ς** of Proclus (referring to page 302, line 3); on page 70, line 16, the phrase "divine silence" (*tēn theian siōpēn*) refers to page 303, line 8, "the sort of silence" (*tēn hoion siōpēn*) in manuscript **C**, where manuscripts **P**, **N**, and **ς** read "the way of silence" (or "seat," or "guide").

I decided these matters should be mentioned in advance. As for a discussion on Proclus’ grammar and style, if such is needed, I have deferred it to the preface of the third volume: I hope, however, that including the most significant points in the indexes will be sufficient.

Regarding the text of Plato’s *Timaeus*—both the version handed down in the manuscripts of Plato and the one found in the lemmas <note>A 'lemma' is the excerpt of the original text placed at the head of a commentary</note> and commentaries of Proclus—I have discussed this more extensively in the *Rheinisches Museum* <note>A prominent German journal for classical philology</note>, volume 58 (1903), page 246 and following. From that dissertation, you will learn, among other things, why I favored the fuller lemmas against the evidence of manuscript **C**, and why I compared certain books of Plato (**A b 𝔗 F 𝔳**) in the apparatus in preference to others.

It remains to give thanks. Above all, with a most grateful heart, I call upon the originator of this edition, **HERMANN DIELS**, who, having recognized the excellence of the manuscripts, not only entrusted the task of editing this commentary to me but was also a constant supporter and encourager of my studies.

The **ROYAL PRUSSIAN MINISTRY OF PUBLIC INSTRUCTION** did not deny financial assistance for my journey to Italy.

Through the generosity and munificence of the **ROYAL BERLIN ACADEMY**, it has been possible for these commentaries to be brought to light and printed.

# CHAPTER TWO

You have seen that the manuscripts of Proclus <note>referring to Proclus Lycaeus (412–485 AD), the influential Neoplatonist philosopher</note> are scattered through many libraries: those who are in charge of these institutions have supported my current studies with the most benevolent spirits—two names, however, I cannot pass over in silence: Salomone Morpurgo of Venice and Henri Omont of Paris—who kindly sent manuscripts to me while I was away. This was made possible through the mediation and characteristic generosity of the directors of the Royal Bavarian Library and the various Bavarian and foreign ministries whenever they were petitioned.

In the cleaning of the proofs <note>original: "plagulis purgandis," referring to the correction of printer's galley proofs</note> and in the restoration of the text, I was assisted with the greatest industry by WILHELM KROLL, highly esteemed in Neoplatonic studies, and LUDWIG RADERMACHER, a distinguished gentleman <note>original: "V. D." likely abbreviating "Vir Doctissimus," most learned man</note>.

Other learned men, when asked by letter, provided me with information: to all of them, I pay my due thanks.

Written at Munich in Bavaria, on the 4th day of the month of October in the year 1903.

->Ernest Diehl<-

## THE COMMENTARIES
## OF PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
## ON THE TIMAEUS OF PLATO

<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) because he headed the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens, succeeding the lineage of Plato.</note>

<summary>The title page for Proclus the Successor's extensive Neoplatonic commentary on Plato's Timaeus, a foundational dialogue concerning the nature of the physical world and the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Plato, Timaeus, Neoplatonism, Commentary, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page contains excerpts from the biography of the Neoplatonist philosopher Proclus, written by his student Marinus. The text focuses on Proclus's early intellectual development and his specific preferences regarding philosophical literature.</meta>

### From Marinus, *The Life of Proclus*

**Chapter 13**
But he <note>Proclus, the subject of the biography</note>, practicing with sleepless discipline and diligence both by night and by day, and recording what was taught concisely and with critical judgment, made such progress in a short time that, while only in his twenty-eighth year, he composed many other works and specifically his commentaries on the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue concerning the creation of the physical world</note>, which are truly elegant and full of scientific knowledge.

**Chapter 38**
Regarding his writings, I will say only this: that he always preferred his commentaries on the *Timaeus* above all others, though he was also very pleased with those on the *Theaetetus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the nature of knowledge</note>. He was also often accustomed to say this: "If I were Lord <note>original: "Kyrios" — implying a position of absolute authority</note>, I would cause only the *Oracles* <note>original: "ta Logia" — referring to the Chaldean Oracles, a sacred Neoplatonic text</note> and the *Timaeus* to be circulated among all the ancient books; the others I would vanish from the men of today, because some are even harmed by encountering them haphazardly and without critical examination."

<summary>These excerpts from Marinus's biography of Proclus highlight the philosopher's prodigious early output, specifically his commentary on Plato's Timaeus, and his belief that only the most sacred and rigorous philosophical texts should be widely available.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marinus, Life of Proclus, Timaeus, Theaetetus, Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, philosophical education, censorship of texts</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page begins the commentary of Proclus on Plato's Timaeus, following the standard Diehl edition (Volume I). It establishes the scope of the work as a comprehensive study of nature.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
# ON THE TIMAEUS OF PLATO
## BOOK ONE

<margin>1</margin>
<margin>5</margin> That the purpose of Plato’s *Timaeus* embraces the <margin>Basel edition</margin> whole of <term>natural philosophy</term><note>original: "physiologia" (φυσιολογία). In this context, it refers to the systematic study of the natural world and its origins, rather than modern biology.</note> and pertains to the contemplation of the universe—treating this subject from beginning to end—seems to me to be perfectly clear to those who are not entirely blinded to these discourses. For indeed, the very writing of the Pythagorean Timaeus, titled *On Nature*, is arranged in the <margin>10</margin> Pythagorean manner. It was from this source that Plato set out and "undertook to write his own Timaeus," according to the <term>writer of satires</term><note>original: "sillographos" (σιλλογράφον). This refers to Timon of Phlius, a skeptical poet known for his satirical verses (Silloi) about philosophers.</note>, a work which we have placed at the beginning of these commentaries. We did this so that we might be able to recognize which things Plato’s *Timaeus* says that are the same as that earlier work, what things he added, and what things are <margin>15</margin> discordant; and so that we might seek the cause of such discordance with more than passing attention. <margin>B</margin>

This entire dialogue, throughout its whole extent, has natural philosophy as its goal, viewing the same realities in both images and in <term>paradigms</term><note>The eternal "models" or "patterns" in the divine mind upon which the physical world is based.</note>, and in both wholes and in parts. <margin>20</margin> For it has been filled with all the most beautiful definitions of natural philosophy, taking up simple things for the sake of the composite, parts for the sake of wholes, and images for the sake of paradigms, leaving nothing unexamined among the primary causes of nature. That the <margin>25</margin> dialogue worthily takes up such a purpose, and that Plato alone, having preserved the Pythagorean character of the contemplation of nature, gave a refined finish to the teaching set before us—this is already clear to those...

---

<note>Critical apparatus notes: Line 6: Kroll suggests "treating" (feminine) to match "purpose." Line 9: See the scholia on Plato 20A regarding the Pythagorean style of writing. Line 11: Reference to Timon, fragment 54. Line 19: Manuscript N reads "parts," while P reads "letters." Line 26: Manuscript N reads "contemplation of nature," while P reads "philosophy."</note>

<summary>Proclus introduces his commentary by asserting that Plato's Timaeus is a complete study of the natural world, modeled on an earlier Pythagorean text. He emphasizes the need to compare Plato's work with its sources to understand his unique contributions to natural philosophy.</summary>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS BOOK I [preface]

...it is necessary to examine those who are more keen-witted. For since the study of nature <note>original: "physiologia"</note> is divided into three parts, to speak concisely: one part occupies itself with **matter** and **material causes**; the second adds the investigation of **form** and declares this to be the more dominant 
<margin>C 5</margin> cause; while the third demonstrates that these [matter and form] do not even hold the rank of causes, but rather of **con-causes** <note>original: "synaitia" - auxiliary factors that allow a cause to act, rather than the primary source of action</note>, and posits other primary causes for things that come into being by nature—namely, the **efficient** <note>the producer</note>, the **paradigmatic** <note>the model or blueprint</note>, and the **final** <note>the purpose</note> causes. Most of the natural philosophers before Plato 
<margin>10</margin> spent their time on matter, some naming one thing and some another as the underlying substrate. For even Anaxagoras—who indeed seems to have seen, while the others were sleeping, that **Mind** <note>original: "Nous"</note> is the cause of all things that come to be—makes no use of Mind in his specific explanations, but rather blames certain "airs" and "ethers" for 
<margin>15</margin> what happens, as Socrates says in the *Phaedo* [98 C]. Those who led the school <note>likely referring to the Peripatetics/Aristotelians</note> after Plato—not all, but at least the more accurate among them—judged it right for the natural philosopher to contemplate the **form** along with the **matter**, reducing the principles of bodies to matter and form. For even if 
<margin>20</margin> they mention the **efficient cause** somewhere, as when they call "nature" a principle of motion, they nevertheless strip it of its active power and its status as a primary producer; they do not allow that there are **rational principles** <note>original: "logoi"</note> within nature for the things produced by it, but instead grant that many things happen 
<margin>D 25</margin> spontaneously. Furthermore, they do not even admit that an efficient cause exists for all natural things without exception, but only for those subject to generation and decay. For they explicitly state that there is no efficient cause for **eternal things**. In this, they either overlook that they are constructing the whole heaven by chance, or they are 
<margin>30</margin> declaring that the body of the world is the producer of itself. But Plato alone, following the Pythagoreans, hands down...

<meta>This page continues Proclus's introductory commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of causes involved in the creation of the physical world.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium]

...the auxiliary causes <note>original: "synaitia"</note> of natural things—namely, the all-receiving substrate <note>matter</note> and the embodied form—which serve the primary causes in the process of generation. But prior to these, he investigates the primary causes: the Producer <note>the Demiurge</note>, the Paradigm <note>the eternal model</note>, and the End <note>the purpose or Good</note>. Because of these, he places over the universe a creative Intellect and an intelligible cause in which the universe exists primarily, as well as the Good, which is established before the Producer in the rank of the "object of desire." For since that which is moved by another depends upon the power of the mover, it is clearly not suited by nature to produce, perfect, or preserve itself; rather, in all these respects, it requires the productive cause and is held together by it.

<margin>E</margin>
Therefore, it is fitting that the auxiliary causes of natural things should also depend on the true causes, from which they are produced, according to which they have been fashioned by the Father of all, and for the sake of which they came into being. It is with good reason, then, that all these causes have been handed down by Plato, examined with precision, and that the remaining two—form and the substrate—are suspended from them. For this world is not the same as the intelligible or intellectual worlds, which subsist in pure forms; rather, there is in this world that which acts as reason-principle <note>term: "logos" - the formative principle that shapes matter</note> and form, and that which acts as substrate. But these matters we shall be able to consider again later. That Plato has rightly handed down all these causes of the world-making <note>original: "kosmopoia"</note> is evident from these: the Good, the intelligible Paradigm, the Producer, the form, and the underlying nature <note>matter</note>. For if he were discussing the intelligible gods, he would have shown the Good to be their only cause; for from this cause alone comes the intelligible number. If he were discussing the intellectual gods, he would have posited both the Good and the intelligible cause for them; for the intellectual multitude proceeds from the intelligible unities <note>term: "henads" - the highest divine principles of oneness</note> and from the single fountain of all beings.

<margin>2</margin>
And if he were discussing the hyper-cosmic <note>above the physical world</note> gods, he would have brought them forth from the intellectual and universal craftsmanship, from the intelligible gods, and from the Cause of all things. For of all things that the secondary causes generate, that Cause is primarily, ineffably, and unimaginably the sustainer. But since he is about to discuss the intra-cosmic <note>within the physical world</note> things and about...

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium]

...of the entire universe, he <note>The Demiurge or Creator</note> will grant it matter and form, sent down into it from the super-cosmic gods; he will link it to the whole of creation, liken it to the Intelligible Living Being <note>original: "noēton zōon" — the divine blueprint of all life</note>, and reveal it as a god through its participation in the Good. In this way, he will render the entire world a god, endowed with intellect and soul.

Such then is the Goal <note>original: "skopos"</note> which we claim *Timaeus* aims for, and it is of the nature we have described. Since this is the case, it is fitting that at the beginning, the order of the universe is demonstrated through images; in the middle, the entire process of world-making <term>cosmopoeia: the creation or ordering of the world</term> is handed down; and toward the end, the particular parts and the final results of the creation are woven together with the wholes.

<margin>B</margin> For the recap of the *Republic* and the myth concerning *Atlantis* reveal the contemplation of the world through images. For whether we look at the unity and the multiplicity of things within the world, we shall say that the *Republic*—which Socrates summarizes—is an image of unity, having established as its goal the communal fellowship that extends through all things; whereas the war of the *Atlanteans* against the *Athenians*, which Critias narrates, is an image of division, and especially of the opposition between the two "coordinates" <note>original: "systoichias" — Pythagorean columns of opposites like Light/Dark or Limit/Unlimited</note>. Or, if we look at the distinction between the heavenly and the sublunary <note>The region below the moon, characterized by change</note>, we shall say that the *Republic* is likened to the heavenly arrangement—for Socrates himself says [Republic IX 592 B] that its model is established in heaven—while the war of the Atlanteans is likened to the realm of generation, which subsists through opposition and change. It is for these reasons, then, that these accounts precede the entire natural philosophy.

Following these, the Creative Cause of the universe is handed down, along with the Exemplary <note>The Model</note> and the Final Cause. With these pre-existing, the universe is created both as a whole and in its parts. For its bodily form is crafted, being divided by forms, creative sections, and 
<margin>C</margin> divine numbers; and the Soul is produced by the Creator <term>Demiurge: the divine craftsman</term> and is filled with harmonic ratios and divine and creative symbols; and the Living Being as a whole is joined...

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium]

The fabric of the world is woven according to the unified enclosure of the intelligible <note>original: "noētos" — the realm of pure thought or forms</note> world, and the parts within it are duly arranged within the whole, both those that are bodily and those that are vital. For indeed, individual souls are assigned their places around the leading gods and become worldly <margin>5</margin> according to their own vehicles <note>The "ochēma" or "astral body" that carries the soul</note>, imitating those who lead them; and mortal living beings are fashioned and given life by the celestial gods. Here, indeed, the human being is also contemplated—how he was constituted and through what causes—and he is considered before all others, <margin>10</margin> either because the contemplation of him is appropriate for us who have put forward the human reason <note>original: "logos"</note> and live according to it, or because the human being is a **microcosm** <note>original: "mikros kosmos" — a small world</note> and contains within himself in a partial way everything that exists in the world in a divine and universal way. For we possess an active intellect <note>original: "nous"</note>, and a rational soul that has proceeded from the same father <margin>15</margin> and the same life-giving goddess <note>Likely referring to Rhea or Hecate in the Neoplatonic system</note> as the whole soul, and an ethereal vehicle analogous to the heavens, and an earthly body mixed from the four elements, to which it is coordinate. If, therefore, it was necessary to contemplate the All in many ways—in the intelligible and in the sensible, as a paradigm, as an image, universally, and partially—it would <margin>20</margin> be well for the account concerning human nature to be perfectly worked out within the contemplation of the All. You might also mention that other reason: that according to the Pythagorean custom, it was necessary to join the account of the observer to the account of the thing observed. For since we have learned what the world is, I believe it was necessary to add <margin>25</margin> what that thing is which examines these matters and understands them through reason. That he [Plato] also looked toward this, he showed clearly at the end [90D], saying that the one who intends to attain the blessed life must make the observer like the thing observed. For the whole is always blessed; <margin>30</margin> and our part will also be blessed when it is made like the All. For in this way, it will be led back to its Cause. For as the human being here is to the All, so also is the Intelligible Human to the...

# 6 PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [preface]

If the Living-Being-in-Itself <note>original: "autozoon" — the divine archetype of all life</note> exists, and in that realm the secondary things are always suspended from the primary, and the parts of the wholes are inseparable and established within them, then whenever the human being here below is made like the universe, he will also imitate his own model in the appropriate manner, <margin>5</margin> becoming orderly <note>original: "kosmios" — a play on "kosmos"</note> through his likeness to the Cosmos and happy through his resemblance to the happy God.

Following these points, the final products of the craftsmanship <note>original: "demiurgia"</note> have been treated in detail according to both genera and species: those things constituted in the heavens, those on the earth, and those in living beings—both those that are contrary to nature and those according to nature. It is here that the principles of medicine come to light; for the natural philosopher <note>original: "physikos"</note> concludes his study at this point, since he is a spectator of nature. For along with nature comes that which is "according to nature," and the departure from this is that which is "contrary to nature." <margin>15</margin> In how many ways this deviation occurs, and how it is led back to due measure and to nature, is the task of the natural philosopher to understand, while the consequences of these things are for the art of medicine to weave together.

In these matters especially, Plato shares common ground with the other natural philosophers. For they occupied themselves <margin>F</margin> with the most material things and the final works of nature, leaving aside the whole heaven and the ranks of the encosmic <note>world-dwelling</note> gods, since they looked only at matter and bid farewell to the forms and the primary causes. <margin>20</margin> It seems to me that even the divine Aristotle, having emulated Plato’s teaching to the best of his ability, arranged his entire treatise on nature in this way: <margin>25</margin> observing the common features of all things constituted by nature—form, substrate, the source of the beginning of motion, motion itself, time, and place—which Plato also handed down here: extension, and time which is the image of eternity and co-exists with the heaven, the various kinds of motion, and the auxiliary causes of natural things. Then he observed the specific features <margin>30</margin> of things divided by substance: first, those appropriate to the heaven, in harmony with Plato, insofar as the heaven is established as ungenerated and consisting of a fifth substance <note>the ether or quintessence</note>—for what...

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium] 7

Is there a difference between calling it a "fifth element" <note>original: "pempton stoicheion"</note> rather than a "fifth world" or a "fifth shape," as Plato did? — Second are the matters common to the entire generative constitution <note>The realm of "becoming" or physical generation</note>, where one might truly admire Plato for having contemplated their essences and powers with great precision, and for correctly preserving both their harmony and their oppositions. Furthermore, regarding these matters of generation, some pertain to celestial phenomena <note>original: "meteōrois"</note>, for which Plato handed down the principles, while Aristotle extended the teaching further than was necessary. <margin>B</margin> Other matters pertain to the study of living beings; in Plato, these are articulated according to all causes—both the final <note>The purpose or "end" of a thing</note> and the auxiliary causes—whereas in Aristotle, they are contemplated only with difficulty and in few instances according to their Form <note>The ideal or essential nature</note>. For in most cases, Aristotle stops at the level of matter, and by making his explanations of natural things from this basis, he shows us how far he falls short of the guidance of our Leader <note>Proclus refers to Plato as "the Leader" or "the Guide"</note>. So much, then, for these matters.

Next, let us discuss the form of the dialogue and what its character is like. It is agreed by all that Plato, having taken the book of the Pythagorean Timaeus which was composed by him concerning the universe, set out to "write a Timaeus" <note>original: "timaiographein"</note> in the Pythagorean manner. Moreover, it is also agreed by those who have even a slight acquaintance with Plato that his own character is Socratic, philanthropic, and demonstrative. If, therefore, he ever blended the Pythagorean and Socratic peculiarities elsewhere, he clearly does so in this dialogue as well. For there is present in it, from the Pythagorean habit, <margin>C</margin> the lofty-minded, the intellectual, the divinely inspired, the quality of suspending all things from the Intelligibles <note>The higher realm of pure thought/Forms</note>, the defining of the whole of things in numbers, the indicating of realities symbolically and mystically, the uplifting, the transcending of partial applications, and the authoritative style. From Socratic philanthropy, on the other hand, comes the ease of conversation, the gentleness, the demonstrative method, and the...

<summary>Proclus argues that Plato’s Timaeus is superior to Aristotle’s physics because it addresses final causes rather than just matter. He describes the dialogue as a perfect synthesis of Pythagorean mysticism and Socratic dialectic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary (prooemium) by Proclus on Plato's Timaeus, focusing on the methodological and theological character of the dialogue.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium]

...contemplating realities through images, the ethical aspect, and all such things. For this reason, the dialogue is indeed solemn; it makes its approaches from above, starting from the <margin>5</margin> very first principles. It mingles the demonstrative with the assertive, and it prepares us to understand physical matters not only physically, but also theologically. For nature itself, which guides the whole, is suspended from the gods and, being inspired by them, directs the bodily realm. It is neither a god itself, nor is it outside the divine character, because it is illuminated by the truly existing gods. If, then, it is necessary for <margin>10</margin> discourses to be like the things of which they are the interpreters—as Timaeus himself will say [29 B]—it would be fitting for this dialogue also to possess both a physical and a theological character, imitating the Nature of which it is a spectator. <margin>D</margin> Furthermore, since according to the Pythagorean pleasure <note>original: "Pythagorean liking/doctrine"</note> things have been divided into three—into the intelligible, the physical, and the intermediates between these, <margin>15</margin> which they are accustomed to call "mathematical"—one can observe all things in all things in an appropriate manner. For in the intelligibles, both the intermediate and the last things pre-exist in a primary way; and in the mathematicals, both are present—the first things as images, and the <margin>20</margin> third things as patterns <note>original: "paradigmatically"</note>; and in physical things, there are reflections of the things prior to them. It is reasonable, then, that Timaeus, in constituting the <gloss>and</gloss> soul through mathematical names, indicates its powers, its ratios, and its elements; while Plato <margin>25</margin> defines its properties from geometric shapes and leaves the causes of all these things pre-existing in a principle-like way within the intelligible and demiurgic <term>Demiurgic Mind: The divine "Craftsman" or intellect that shapes the cosmos according to eternal patterns.</term> mind. Regarding these matters, let this suffice, as the investigation of particulars is better able to acquaint us with the character of the dialogue.

The hypothesis <note>The "plot" or setting of the dialogue</note> is something like this: Socrates, having <margin>30</margin> arrived at the Piraeus <margin>E</margin> for the sake of the festival of Bendis <note>A Thracian goddess whose festival involved a torch race</note> and the procession, conversed there about the Republic with Polemarchus the son of Cephalus...

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Introduction]

...Cephalus, and Glaucon, and Adeimantus, and indeed Thrasymachus the sophist. But on the day following this one, in the city <note>Athens proper, as opposed to the Piraeus harbor</note>, he relates the gathering that took place in the Piraeus to Timaeus, Hermocrates, Critias, and a fourth anonymous person besides these, just as is set forth in the *Republic*. Having finished his narration, he invited the others to feast him in return with their own appropriate discourses on the following day. They have come together, therefore, both to listen and to speak on this day, which is the third from the gathering in the Piraeus. For in the *Republic* <insert>[I 327 A]</insert> it is said, "I went down yesterday," and here <insert>[17 A]</insert> it mentions "those who were guests yesterday, but are hosts today." However, not everyone is present for this hearing; the fourth person is absent due to illness. Why then, one might ask, are there three listeners in a work where the discourse concerns the entire universe? Because, I shall say, it is fitting for the father of the discourses to stand in analogy to the Father of the works <note>The Demiurge or Creator</note>; for the world-making according to reason is an image of the world-making according to Intellect. To the receiving demiurgic triad <note>A group of three divine powers in Neoplatonic theology</note> which accepts the single and total creation of the Father, there corresponds the triad of those receiving the discourses. The summit of this triad is Socrates, who, through a kinship of life, immediately joins himself to Timaeus, just as the first member of the Paradigmatic <term>triad</term> <note>The realm of Forms used as a model for creation</note> is united with the One who stands before the three. We shall press these points more clearly through the following sections, if it be pleasing to the gods.

<margin>F</margin> Having already spoken concerning the scope—what it is and how great it is—and the arrangement of the dialogue, and the character of the style within it (which is wonderfully blended), and the whole hypothesis, and indeed concerning the suitability of the persons for the present discourses, it would be fitting to proceed to the text itself and examine each point to the best of our ability. But since the name of "Nature," being used differently by different people, confuses those who love to behold the mind of Plato...
<margin>4</margin>

<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary (prooemium) by Proclus on Plato's Timaeus, specifically investigating the definition of "nature."</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium]

Since it is necessary to understand what he <note>Plato</note> thinks nature is, and what he wishes the essence of nature to be, come, let us first discuss this matter. For it would be fitting for a study that has physical theory as its object to know what nature is, from where it proceeds, and how far its creative powers extend. For among the ancients, some called matter <note>original: "hyle"</note> nature, such as Antiphon; others called it the form <note>original: "eidos"</note>, as Aristotle does in many places; others called it the whole, as did some of those before Plato, whom he recorded in the *Laws* [X 892 B], because they called natural things "natures"; others called it natural powers, such as heaviness, lightness, rarefaction, and condensation, as <margin>10</margin> did some of the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle's school</note> and even older natural philosophers; others named nature the "art of God"; others called it soul; and others something else of this kind. 

But Plato does not think that matter, or the embodied form, or the body, or natural powers deserve to be called nature in the primary sense. Yet he also hesitates to call it soul directly. Instead, he places its essence in the middle of both—I mean between soul and bodily powers. It is inferior to the soul because it is divided among bodies and does not return to itself <note>A Neoplatonic concept where higher entities are self-aware and self-contained, whereas nature is directed outward into matter</note>, but it surpasses the things that come after it by possessing the formative principles <note>term: "logoi" — the underlying rational blueprints of physical things</note> of all things, and by generating and giving life to everything. In this way, he has handed down to us the most accurate theory concerning it. 

For according to common notions, nature is one thing, and that which is "according to nature" or "by nature" is another—just as the artificial object is different from the art itself. Furthermore, the intellectual soul is one thing and nature is another. <margin>25</margin> For nature belongs to bodies, sinking into them and being inseparable from them, whereas the soul is separable, established in itself, and belongs both to itself and to another: it belongs to another by being participated in, but belongs to itself by not inclining toward that which participates in it. Just as the father of the soul <note>The Demiurge or Creator</note> belongs only to himself, being unparticipated <note>term: "amethektos" — a principle that remains pure and does not mix with lower levels of reality</note>, and, if you wish, even before him, the Intelligible Paradigm <note>The divine model or blueprint</note> of the entire cosmos itself. For these things are held...

---

<summary>Proclus reviews various ancient definitions of "nature" (matter, form, divine art) before explaining Plato's specific view of nature as an intermediary force between the rational soul and the physical body.</summary>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's introductory commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the hierarchy of causes and the nature of "Nature" (Physis) as a mediating force between the divine and the material.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Prooemium]

...of one another: the same, that which belongs to itself, that which belongs to itself and another, that which belongs to another, and the other. It is clear that this last one refers to the entire sensible world <note>The physical world perceived by the senses</note>, in which there is extension and every kind of division. Of the former categories, one is Nature, which is inseparable from bodies; another is the Soul, which exists within itself while also illuminating a second life into another; another is the Demiurgic Intellect <note>The "Craftsman" or Creator Mind in Platonic thought</note>, remaining in its own accustomed character [Timaeus 42 E]; and another is the Intelligible Cause of all things, the paradigm <note>The perfect model or blueprint</note> of those things produced by the Creator, which Plato for this reason saw fit to call the "Living-Being-in-Itself" [30 CD].

Nature, then, is the lowest of the causes that craft this body-like and sensible world, and the limit of the extent of incorporeal substances. She is full of "reasons" <note>original: "logoi" — formative principles or seeds of reality</note> and powers through which she directs the things of the cosmos. She is indeed a god, but by being "deified" and not by possessing her divine being from herself—for we also call the divine bodies <note>The stars and planets</note> gods, as they are images of the gods. She guides 
<margin>D</margin> 
the whole world by her own powers, holding the heavens together by her highest point, governing the realm of generation <note>The world of change and birth</note> through the heavens, and everywhere weaving partial things together with the wholes. Being such as she is, she has proceeded forth from the Life-generating Goddess <note>Likely referring to Rhea or Hecate in the Neoplatonic pantheon</note>:

> And around the back of the Goddess, boundless Nature is suspended;

from whom all life proceeds, both the intellectual life and that which is inseparable from the things it manages. Hanging from that source and suspended from it, Nature wanders through all things without hindrance and breathes life into all. Because of her, even the most soulless things participate in a kind of soul, and things that perish remain eternally in the cosmos, held together by the causes of the Forms <note>The eternal archetypes</note> within her.

"And tireless Nature rules over the worlds and works," says the Oracle <note>Referring to the Chaldean Oracles, a sacred text for Neoplatonists</note>,

> so that the Heaven may run its eternal course, drawing along...

---

<meta>Critical Apparatus notes: The text cites various manuscript traditions (M, N, P) and references the Chaldean Oracles and Orphic fragments.</meta>

<summary>Proclus defines Nature (Physis) as the lowest incorporeal cause that governs the physical world, acting as a bridge between the divine Intellect and material reality. He describes Nature as a "deified" force originating from a higher Life-generating Goddess, ensuring the continuity of the cosmos.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Timaeus, Nature, Physis, Demiurge, Chaldean Oracles, Neoplatonism, Forms, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the prooemium (preface) of Proclus' Commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the nature of the Demiurge and the role of Nature (Physis) in creation.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [preface]

...and so forth. Consequently, if anyone who speaks of three demiurges <note>The "Demiurge" is the divine craftsman or creator figure in Platonic thought.</note> wishes to refer them back to these principles—the demiurgic intellect, the soul, and total nature—he would speak correctly for the reasons stated. But if he posits some other three demiurges of the universe beyond the soul, he is <margin>E</margin> incorrect; for the demiurge of the whole is one, though more partial powers have partitioned his total demiurgic work. Therefore, whether Amelius <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher, student of Plotinus.</note> wishes to arrange things in this way or Theodore <note>Theodore of Asine, a student of Iamblichus known for complex metaphysical systems.</note>, we do not accept the argument, but we shall strive to remain upon the Platonic and Orphic <note>Referring to the mystical teachings attributed to the legendary poet Orpheus, which Neoplatonists integrated with Plato.</note> hypotheses. Furthermore, as many as have called Nature a "demiurgic art" <term>techne: craft or systematic art</term>: if they mean the art remaining within the demiurge himself, they do not speak correctly; but if they mean the art proceeding from him, they are right. For one must conceive of art in a threefold way: one that is un-departing from the craftsman, one that proceeds but turns back toward him, and one that has already proceeded and come to be in another. Now, the art in the demiurge remains in him and is his very self, according to which he is also named "Master-Craftsman" <note>original: "ergotechnites"</note> by the Oracles <note>The Chaldean Oracles, a 2nd-century sacred text central to late Neoplatonism.</note> and "Craftsman of the Fiery World." The intellectual soul is indeed an art, but one that both remains and proceeds at the same time; but Nature is that which has only proceeded. On this account, it is also called an "instrument of the gods," not as something lifeless or moved only by another, but as possessing a kind of self-motion by acting from itself. For the instruments of the gods are substantialized in active principles <term>logoi: formative or rational principles</term> and are vital and concurrent <margin>F</margin> with their activities.

Since, therefore, it has been stated what Nature is according to Plato—that it is an incorporeal substance, inseparable from bodies, containing their formative principles, and unable to look into itself—and since it is clear from these points how the dialogue is "physical" <note>i.e., dealing with Physis/Nature.</note> as it teaches about the entire making of the world, it would be appropriate to connect what follows to these points.

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Proclus' Commentary on Plato's Timaeus, establishing the relationship between the Timaeus and the Parmenides.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [prooemium]

For since the whole of philosophy is divided into the contemplation of the <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: "noēta" — things grasped by the mind rather than the senses</note> and the contemplation of the <term>intramundane</term> <note>original: "egkosmia" — things existing within the physical cosmos</note>, and reasonably so, because the world is also twofold—the one intelligible and the other sensible, as he himself will say [30 C] as he proceeds—Parmenides has embraced the study of the intelligibles, while Timaeus has embraced the study of intramundane things. For the former transmits all the divine orders, while the latter transmits all the progressions of intramundane things. Yet neither does that one <note>Parmenides</note> entirely omit the contemplation of the things in the universe, nor does this one <note>Timaeus</note> omit that of the intelligibles, because sensible things exist in the intelligibles <margin>10</margin> as <term>paradigms</term> <note>original: "paradeigmatikōs" — as original models</note> and the intelligibles exist in the sensibles as <term>images</term> <note>original: "eikonikōs"</note>. But the one abounds in the physical, and the other in the <term>theological</term> <note>original: "theologikon" — here meaning the study of the highest principles or gods</note>, appropriately to the men after whom they are named; for a certain writing of this kind had been written by Timaeus concerning the nature of the universe, and by Parmenides concerning truly existing things. Correctly, <margin>15</margin> therefore, says the divine Iamblichus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher who influenced Proclus</note> that the whole of Plato’s contemplation is contained in these two dialogues, the *Timaeus* and the *Parmenides*; for the entire study of intramundane and <term>supramundane</term> <note>original: "hyperkosmia" — things beyond the physical world</note> things has its best end in them, and no order of beings has been left uninvestigated. It would appear <margin>20</margin> <margin>B</margin> to those who do not encounter them casually that the method of the study in the *Timaeus* is most similar to that in the *Parmenides*. For just as Timaeus traces the cause of all things in the world back to the very first <term>Demiurge</term> <note>original: "dēmiourgos" — the divine Craftsman or Creator</note>, so Parmenides attaches the progression of all beings to the One. And the former transmits how all things participate in the demiurgic providence, while the latter transmits how beings have shared in the "one-like" existence. Furthermore, just as Timaeus, before the physiology <note>The study of nature</note>, proffers the contemplation of intramundane things through images, so Parmenides stirs up the inquiry concerning the immaterial <term>Forms</term> <note>original: "eidōn"</note> before the theology. <margin>30</margin> For it is necessary, having exercised oneself in the discourses concerning the best state, to be led up to the understanding of the universe, and having struggled in the intense difficulties concerning the Forms...

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion of Proclus' commentary on Plato's Timaeus, transitioning from the general theological purpose of the dialogue to the specific analysis of the opening lines.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

...to be sent up toward the mystical contemplation of the unities <note>original: "henads" (ἑνάδων). In Neoplatonism, these are the highest divine principles above even Being itself.</note>. Now that these things have been said, it would be the right time to take hold of Plato’s text and examine each part as best we are able.

<margin>C</margin>->**One, two, three—but where, my dear**<-
->**Timaeus, is the fourth of our guests of yesterday, who are**<-
->**now our hosts? [17 A]**<-

Longinus the critic <note>A famous 3rd-century rhetorician and philosopher</note>, applying a philological eye to this speech, says that it is composed of three clauses. He notes that the first clause is somewhat plain and common, but because of the way the interpretation is resolved, the second clause is rendered more magnificent through the variation of nouns and the continuity of the words. Even more so, the third clause adds both grace and sublimity to both. For the phrase "one, two, three," being composed of disconnected words, made the speech flat. But the following part—"but where, my dear Timaeus, is the fourth"—rendered the expression more venerable, both through the word "fourth" being varied against the previously mentioned numbers and through being joined with magnificent names. Finally, the phrase "guests of yesterday, but now our hosts," through the grace and beauty of the words and the use of metaphor, raised and exalted the entire period.

However, Praxiphanes <note>A Peripatetic philosopher and student of Theophrastus</note>, the companion of Theophrastus, brings charges against Plato. First, he complains that Plato assigned the "one, two, three" to Socrates when the count was already obvious and known to the senses; for why did Socrates need to count in order to know the number of those who had arrived for the gathering? Second, he complains that he varied the word "fourth" and did not use it in harmony with the preceding words; for "four" would follow "one, two, three," whereas "fourth" should follow "first, second, third." Such is the "myth" <note>Proclus uses "myth" here dismissively to mean a trivial or baseless story</note> of that man. But the philosopher Porphyry <note>The famous student of Plotinus and editor of the Enneads</note> answers him step by step: regarding the second point, he says that this is a feature of Greek syn-

Homer has said many such things <note>referring to Iliad 7.247</note>: for he says that
"through six folds went the unwearying bronze,
but it was held in the seventh"; and he speaks in this way with the name
<margin>5</margin> and in many other places. However, the variation here also has a cause: <margin>D</margin>
for it was necessary to count those present by pointing to them—
for "one, two, three" is demonstrative—but the one who is absent—since
it was impossible to point to him—he indicated through the word "fourth."
For we use "fourth" in the case of one who is absent. Furthermore, regarding the former point,
<margin>10</margin> since there were as many present as was reasonable to arrive,
counting them would be superfluous; but when someone is missing, whom we do not know
by name, the enumeration of those present gives an indication of the one missing,
as if longing for the remainder and as if falling short
of the whole number. Therefore, Plato, in demonstrating this,
<margin>15</margin> has made Socrates both count those
who have arrived and demand the one who is left behind. For if
he knew that person and it were possible to declare him by name,
he would perhaps have said that he sees Critias and Timaeus and Her-
mocrates, but does not see "so-and-so." But since the absent one was a stranger
<margin>20</margin> and unknown to him, he both knows that he is away through the number alone,
and makes it clear to us who have come so much later.
Now, all these points are graceful, as are any such things
one might devise for the contemplation of the passage at hand;
but one must also remember that the dialogue is Pythagorean, and it is necessary <margin>E</margin>
<margin>25</margin> to make the explanations in the manner appropriate to them. Therefore,
you might take from here such Pythagorean ethical doctrines as these:
those men set friendship as the goal of all
their philosophy, and the life of shared harmony <term>homonoētikē zōē: a life lived in intellectual and social concord</term>. This indeed
Socrates has put forward before all else, calling Timaeus "friend."
<margin>30</margin> They thought that the agreements they made with one another
ought to remain firm for them; these also Socrates...

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary by Proclus on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the significance of the numbers and the social character of the dialogue.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

...he demands the fourth, longing for him. They cherished the fellowship found in the discovery of doctrines, and the writings of one were the common property of all. Socrates confirms this by calling upon the same guests and hosts to be present, both filling others and being filled themselves, both teaching and learning. Now, others have written technical treatises on duties <note>original: "kathēkontōn" — referring to Stoic ethics regarding appropriate actions</note>, through which they claim to improve the characters of those educated by them; but Plato, through the very imitation <note>term: "mimēsis" — the act of representing or mimicking reality in art or speech</note> of the best men, sketches for us the patterns of duties, which possess a much more effective power than those stored in naked rules. <margin>F</margin> For imitation shapes the lives of the listeners according to its own specific quality. In what area the philosopher will especially abound, he shows through these words: namely, in the hearing of discourses. And what he will consider to be a true feast: not that which the many believe—for that is animalistic—but that which feasts the "human being within us" <note>A reference to Republic IX 589 A, where the rational soul is described as the inner human</note>. Therefore, the term "feasting" is frequently used by him in relation to discourses: *Is it not clear that Lysias feasted you with discourses?* [Phaedr. 227 B]; and *Will you two feast alone, and not give us a share?* [Lys. 211 D]. <margin>6</margin>

These matters, then, and all such things, are ethical; but the physical matters are as follows: they used to say that the entire production of nature was held together by numbers, and that all the handiwork of nature was constituted according to numbers, and that these numbers were participated in, just as all mundane forms are participable. Reasonably, then, the discourse as it begins proceeds through numbers and uses numbers as "things numbered," but not those primary numbers themselves which these others participate in. For the Monad, Dyad, and Triad <note>The abstract principles of One, Two, and Three</note> are one thing, while "one, two, three" are another. For the former are simple and each is itself, while the latter participate in those. Therefore, Aristotle was not correct in saying that these men placed numbers within sensible objects. For how could they? They who hymn Number as the "father of gods and men" and the Tetrad <note>term: "tetraktys" — the Pythagorean "four-fold" sum (1+2+3+4=10), considered the source of eternal nature</note>...

<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically the dialogue's introductory count of guests (1, 2, 3...). Proclus is interpreting these numbers not just as a headcount, but as symbols of physical and divine principles.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

->the fountain of ever-flowing nature;<-
<note>original: "παγὰν ἀενάου φύσεως" — a reference to the Pythagorean oath regarding the Tetraktys.</note>

<margin>B</margin> Since this dialogue is concerned with nature <term>physikos: relating to the study of the natural world</term>, it makes its first approach using participated numbers, which is the method used by all natural philosophers. Furthermore, these men <note>referring to the characters in the dialogue: Socrates, Timaeus, Critias, and Hermocrates</note> honored natural fellowship, <margin>5</margin> both that which exists in the realm of generation—where all things are rendered expressible and commensurate with one another—and that which exists in the celestial realms; for even there, the heavenly bodies share their own powers with one another. It is therefore right and fitting for the subject at hand that Socrates expects the same individuals to be both guests and hosts.

<margin>10</margin> From these things, you might also contemplate theological insights of the following kind. Through these primary and leading numbers, these men brought all things into being, and from the three gods they gave substance to all things within the cosmos. The Monad <term>the number one, representing unity</term>, the Dyad <term>the number two, representing duality</term>, <margin>15</margin> and the Triad <term>the number three</term> serve as indications of these; thus, it is necessary for the future observer of nature to begin from them and look toward them. Furthermore, while the "co-causes" of natural things had been observed by others, the Final, the Paradigmatic, and the Productive causes were investigated specifically by them. These causes are revealed through the numbers set forth: the Final cause through the Monad (for <margin>C</margin> <margin>20</margin> it stands at the head of the numbers in the rank of the Good); the Paradigmatic cause through the Dyad (for the "otherness" of beings distinguished the primary-working causes of the whole; moreover, the Dyad is the principle of the Tetraktys <note>The "four-fold" sum of 1+2+3+4=10, central to Pythagorean thought</note> of the intelligible archetypes); and the <margin>25</margin> Productive cause is signified through the Triad. For the Intellect <term>Nous</term> is properly suited to the Triad, being third from Being by way of Life as the middle, or from the Father by way of Power, or from the Intelligible by way of Intellection. For the proportion holds: as the Monad is to the Dyad, so is Being to Life, the Father to Power, and the Intelligible to Intellection; and as the Dyad is to the Triad, so is <margin>30</margin> Life to Intellect, as are Power and Intellection. Furthermore...

<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the social and spiritual "banquet" of discourse between Socrates and his interlocutors.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

All divine things exist in all others and are united to one another, such that all are in one and each is in all, held together by divine friendship. Even the **Sphere** there <note>original: *Sphairos*. A reference to Empedocles fragment 27, describing the primordial state of the universe as a perfectly unified, divine globe.</note> contains the single union of the gods. It is reasonable, then, that Socrates, looking toward the <margin>D</margin> divine, takes the lead in fellowship and harmony, and invites the others into this state. Furthermore, "banqueting" and "feasting" are names appropriate to the gods, and especially to the celestial gods; for they go up to the unconditioned gods for feast and banquet, as Socrates says in the *Phaedrus* [247 A], and the banquet on the birthday of **Aphrodite** <note>The goddess of love and beauty.</note> takes place at the home of the great **Zeus** <note>The king of the gods; this refers to the myth in Plato's *Symposium*.</note>. Socrates deems it right that these things should happen analogously among them, as they share divine thoughts with one another. And it is no wonder if **Timaeus** feasts the others and is feasted by them, since even among the theologians <note>Proclus often uses "theologians" to refer to ancient poets like Orpheus or the authors of the Chaldean Oracles.</note> there are celebrated transmissions of powers and participations, with divine beings filling one another and being filled by one another. For we have heard from the divinely inspired poets that the gods greet one another with intellectual activities or with providential acts of creation toward the whole:

->And they, with golden cups,<-
->pledged one another as they looked down upon the city of the Trojans [Iliad 4.4].<-

And if they also know and perceive one another:

->For gods are not unknown to one another [Odyssey 5.79].<-

Since the "intelligible" <term>intelligible: that which can be grasped by the mind rather than the senses</term> is the nourishment for the perceiver according to the Oracle <note>A reference to the Chaldean Oracles, a sacred Neoplatonic text.</note>, it is clear <margin>E</margin> that "reciprocal feasting" <note>original: *antaphestian*</note> exists primarily among the gods, and the wiser among men, imitating the gods in this way, share their own thoughts with one another without envy.

**"Some illness has befallen him, Socrates; for he would not willingly have been absent from this gathering" [17 A].**

The philosopher **Porphyry** <note>An earlier Neoplatonic philosopher and student of Plotinus.</note> says it is a duty to describe...

M N P <note>These letters refer to the specific medieval manuscripts—Marcianus, Neapolitanus, and Parisinus—used to reconstruct this text.</note>

<summary>Proclus explains that the social gathering in the Timaeus mimics the divine unity and "reciprocal feasting" of the gods, where intellectual exchange is viewed as a form of sacred nourishment. He uses citations from Homer and Plato to justify the spiritual significance of the dialogue's setting.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Timaeus, Socrates, divine friendship, Empedocles, Sphairos, Aphrodite, Zeus, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Homer, Chaldean Oracles, reciprocal feasting, intellectual nourishment</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary by Proclus on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the absence of the "fourth guest" mentioned at the start of the dialogue.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS BOOK I [Tim. 17 A]

In these lines, he states that for sensible men, the only reason for missing such gatherings is bodily weakness, and that one must consider all such circumstances as accidental and <margin>5</margin> involuntary. Furthermore, he notes that it is the role of friends to make whatever excuses are possible on behalf of their friends whenever they seem to be acting incorrectly <insert>not</insert> <note>original: "οὐκ" - added by modern editors to complete the sense</note> in accordance with a common agreement. At any rate, this passage encompasses both the character of Timaeus—showing him to be gentle and a lover of truth—and the necessity of the one who is absent, which acts as a hindrance to the life of a lover of learning <term>philologos: one devoted to reason and study</term>.

But the divine <margin>10</margin> Iamblichus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) known for his mystical and systematic approach</note>, speaking more loftily on this passage, says that those who have been trained in the contemplation of intelligible realities <term>noēta: things grasped by the mind rather than the senses</term> are disproportionately disposed toward spending time on sensible things. As Socrates himself has said somewhere in the *Republic* [VII 516 E ff.], those raised in the pure <margin>15</margin> light suffer from blurred vision when they descend into the cave because of the darkness there, just as those who have ascended from the cave cannot yet look directly at the light. For this reason, the fourth person is left behind as belonging to another kind of contemplation—that of the intelligibles—and indeed, this "weakness" of his is actually an excess of power, by which he surpasses the <margin>20</margin> present inquiry. For just as the "power" of evil is actually a lack of power, so too weakness toward secondary things is an excellence of power. Thus, the argument says that the one left behind is absent because he is out of proportion with discussions on nature <term>physikoīs logoīs</term>, though he would wish to be present if they were going to spend their time on the intelligibles.

<margin>25</margin> Almost everything preceding the discussion on nature is interpreted by Porphyry <note>A student of Plotinus and a more literal/ethical commentator</note> in a more political or social manner, referring the words to the virtues and so-called duties; but another <note>likely Iamblichus</note> interprets them in a more naturalistic way. For everything must be in harmony with the proposed goal, and this dialogue is about nature <term>physikos</term>, not ethics. These, then, are the arrangements the philosophers <margin>30</margin> make regarding these matters. I shall pass over those who cause us much trouble in order to demonstrate that this fourth person was Theaetetus, simply because he had become an acquaintance of those from the Eleatic <note>Referring to the school of Parmenides and Zeno</note>...

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s commentary on the opening of Plato’s Timaeus, specifically addressing the identity of the "fourth guest" who is absent from the gathering.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

school, and because he described him as being ill; therefore, he is absent now as well due to sickness. For in this way Aristocles <note>A Peripatetic philosopher who wrote on the history of philosophy</note> argues that the absent person is Theaetetus. And yet, 
<margin>B</margin>
Theaetetus became an acquaintance of Socrates and the Eleatic Stranger <note>The unnamed philosopher from Elea who leads the dialogue in Plato's *Sophist*</note> only a short time before Socrates’s death. Even if he had been an acquaintance long before that, what would Timaeus have in common with him? Ptolemy the Platonist, however, thinks the person is Cleitophon; for in the dialogue of the same name, he was not even deemed worthy of a response from Socrates. But Dercyllides <note>A Platonist who worked on the arrangement of Plato's dialogues</note> thinks it is Plato; for he too was absent from the death of Socrates due to illness <note>original: "διὰ νόσον" (dia noson); a reference to *Phaedo* 59 B</note>. These views, as I said, I shall set aside, as they were rightly rejected by those before us for seeking things not worth seeking and for saying nothing certain. For they all attempt their arguments in a strained manner, and it contributes nothing to the discourse even if we were to find what they seek. Furthermore, to say it is Theaetetus or Plato because of illness does not even harmonize with the chronology; for one of these is said to be ill when Socrates was on trial, and the other when he died. As for it being Cleitophon, that is utterly absurd; for he was not even present the day before, when Socrates was recounting <note>original: "διηγουμένου" (diēgoumenou); referring to the *Republic* I 340 AB</note> what Cleitophon had said on the day before that during the gathering in the Piraeus <note>The port of Athens where the *Republic* is set</note>. 
<margin>C</margin>
However, Atticus <note>A 2nd-century Platonist</note> correctly notes this much: it seems this missing person is one of the foreigners who came with Timaeus. This is why Socrates asks Timaeus himself where the fourth might be, and Timaeus offers an excuse for the absence as if for a close friend, showing that it was necessary and involuntary. These, then, are the views of the older commentators. But what our own guide <note>Proclus refers here to his teacher, Syrianus</note> decided in these matters must be stated by us, following Plato with particular care. He used to say, then, that the more the lectures concern more august and sublime matters, the more the number of listeners...

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically analyzing the significance of the number of participants present for the dialogue compared to the Republic.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

...the number of them decreases, while the discourse proceeds in a more mystical and secret <note>original: "ἀπορρητότερον" — referring to "esoteric" or "unspoken" teachings intended for a select few</note> manner. For these reasons, in the first delivery of the *Republic* during the gathering in the Piraeus <note>The setting of Plato's Republic, a bustling port town representing the world of change and many voices</note>, the audience was large, and those specifically named were six. In the second delivery, according to the narration of Socrates, there were four who received the discourse. But in this present dialogue, even the fourth is absent, and there are only three listeners. Indeed, the more pure and intellectual <note>original: "νοερώτερος" — relating to the higher Mind or Intellect (Nous)</note> the discourse becomes, the more the audience is contracted.

For everywhere the Monad <term>Monad: the principle of oneness or unity</term> is the leader. However, where the discourse is competitive <note>original: "ἀγωνιστικῶς" — referring to the dialectical "struggle" or debate found in earlier dialogues</note>, the listeners possess both the indefinite and the definite, extending into a multitude in which the odd is interwoven with the even. But where the discourse is narrative, yet not yet freed from the battle and the dialectical struggles, the listeners are four. This is because the Tetrad <term>Tetrad: the number four</term>, through its square form and its kinship to the Monad, possesses similarity and sameness, yet through the nature of the even number, it possesses difference and multitude.

<margin>D</margin>

Where, however, all competitive instruction is removed, and the contemplation <note>original: "θεωρίας" — the spiritual or philosophical "vision" of truth</note> is woven in a demonstrative and guiding manner, the Triad <term>Triad: the number three</term> is appropriate for those receiving it. The Triad is united with the Monad in all things, being odd, primary, and perfect.

For just as some virtues exist among those who are fighting and they measure their battle, while others separate the soul from the fighters but have not yet entirely departed from them, and still others are found to be completely separated—so too among discourses: some are competitive, some are demonstrative, and some are in the middle between both. Some belong to the intellectual stillness and the intuition <note>original: "νοήσει"</note> of the soul, others to the activities of opinion <note>original: "δοξαστικαῖς" — relating to 'doxa' or common belief</note>, and others to the lives between these two. And indeed, among listeners, some are proportioned to the higher teachings, while others are suited to those that are more earth-bound. Those who are listeners of the greater things are also able to meet the lower ones, but those naturally suited to the lesser are unable to reach the solemn...

<margin>E</margin>

<summary>Proclus explains why the number of Socrates's listeners decreases from the Republic to the Timaeus, arguing that as philosophy becomes more "mystical" and "intellectual," the audience must become smaller and more unified, moving from the "multitude" to the "Triad."</summary>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus’s commentary on the opening of Plato’s Timaeus (17a), specifically addressing the absence of the "fourth guest" from the previous day’s discussion on the Republic.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

...marvel. Thus also among the virtues <term>aretē; excellence or moral virtue</term>, the one who possesses the greater also possesses the lesser, but the one adorned with the inferior virtues has not necessarily attained the more perfect ones. Why then is it still a matter of wonder if one who has heard the discourses on the State <margin>5</margin> <note>Referring to Plato's *Republic*</note> is left behind by the hearing concerning the Universe? Rather, how is it not necessary that in the deeper discourses, those following along should be fewer? And how is it not Pythagorean to have defined different measures for those listening? For indeed, among those who attended the "common-hearing" <note>original: *homakoeion*; the school or assembly of Pythagoras</note>, some grasped deeper doctrines and others more superficial ones. <margin>10</margin> <margin>F</margin> And how is this not in harmony with Plato, who blamed "weakness" for the absence? For the inability of the soul <term>psychē</term> to grasp the more divine thoughts keeps us away from the higher gatherings. Here, indeed, the "involuntary" has its place; for everything that benefits us more greatly is voluntary, while the falling away from more perfect things is involuntary. Or rather, the latter is "not-voluntary," while that which not only stands apart from the greater goods but is also carried down into the boundlessness of vice is "involuntary." <margin>15</margin> <note>Proclus distinguishes between *ouch hekousion* (not-voluntary/neutral) and *akousion* (involuntary/against the will).</note> Therefore, Timaeus says that the fourth guest is not left behind from this gathering by choice; for he is not left behind as one who is entirely averse to contemplation <term>theōria</term>, but as one who is unable to be initiated into the greater mysteries. <margin>20</margin> While it is possible for the listener of the discourses on the creation of the world to also listen to those on the State, it is among the impossibilities for one who [only] endured the discourses on the State to be left behind by the hearings concerning the Universe because of an "excess of power." <margin>25</margin> <margin>8</margin> It is therefore through deficiency and not through superiority—as some claim—that the fourth guest is left behind from the present discourses. One must describe this weakness not as an asymmetry of the others toward him, but as his own inferiority compared to the others. For let there be "inability" both in the case of those descending from the Intelligible <note>The realm of pure forms/ideas</note> and those being led up from the contemplation of sensible things, such as Socrates recorded in the *Republic* [VII 518 AB]; but the listener of political discourses... <margin>30</margin>
