--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the significance of the missing fourth guest and the mathematical symbolism of numbers.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 A]

having become such, he would not, by some unknown excellence, be left out of the contemplation of natural philosophy by those present. And the phrase "it was an accident" <note>original: "συνέπεσε"</note> suggests his inferiority compared to those present, rather than any superiority, or so I believe. Furthermore, <margin>5</margin> the fact that he is "anonymous" <note>unnamed</note> does not seem to signify something transcendent or indescribable to those present, but rather the indefinite <margin>B</margin> and deficient nature of his character. Indeed, Plato is accustomed to doing this in many places; for instance, in the *Phaedo* [103 A], he did not even deign to give a name to the one who raised a poor objection, and he grouped the father of Critobulus <margin>10</margin>—as one falling short of the discussions then taking place—and many others together indefinitely. Such a listener would have attended these discourses in vain, seeing as even among those present, Critias himself says something, while Hermocrates is present in silence, differing from the absent one only <margin>15</margin> insofar as he is more fit for listening, yet falling short of all the others in his ability to speak.

->**"It is therefore the task of you who are here to fulfill the part of the one who is absent." [17 A]**<-

And these things harmonize with what we have said: for <margin>20</margin> in the more causal and more divine things, the quantity is always contracted and the plurality is diminished, but the power is transcendent. This is also a doctrine of the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras who believed numbers were the essence of all things</note>, among whom the triad <note>the number three</note> is more august than the tetrad <note>the number four</note>, and the tetrad more than the decad <note>the number ten</note>, and all numbers within the decad are superior to those after it. Simply put, that which is closer <margin>25</margin> to the Beginning <note>the Principle or Source</note> is more "principle-like," and that which is more principle-like is more powerful, <margin>C</margin> since all power is pre-contained in the Beginning and is given by the Beginning to all others. If, then, the Beginning were a plurality, it would follow that the more pluralized thing would be more principle-like and powerful than the less; but since the Beginning is a Monad <note>The One; the ultimate unit</note>, <margin>30</margin> that which is more "monad-like" is better and more powerful than those things which stand further apart from the Cause. Reasonably, then, Socrates makes the reduction of the number <note>from four guests to three</note> a symbol of a higher perfection, which pre-contains all secondary things according to its power, and [fills] that which is lacking...

<summary>Proclus argues that the missing fourth guest in the Timaeus represents a deficiency rather than a hidden superiority, using Pythagorean numerology to explain why smaller numbers (like three) are more powerful and "divine" than larger ones.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Timaeus, Plato, Pythagoras, Monad, Triad, Tetrad, Numerology, Socrates, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the relationship between the speakers (Socrates, Timaeus, Critias, and Hermocrates) and their symbolic roles in the Neoplatonic hierarchy.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 AB]

...completing their number. Since, as has been said before, he <note>Timaeus</note> is the summit of this triad of listeners, and [Socrates] joins him to the Monad <note>The "One" or primary principle</note> who arranged the discourses according to the image of the demiurgic <term>demiurgic: relating to the Creator or Craftsman of the universe</term> gods, it is worth observing how he sets Timaeus apart from the others. He addresses him as the director of the entire gathering, yet ranks the others with him as being subordinate to his dignity. For these details might lead us back to the divine causes, in which the first member of the Triad, being united to the primary Monad, also extends the others toward her; he calls forth the production of the Monad and awakens the activities of the others toward generation. 

<margin>D</margin> These points follow from what was said previously. However, Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> records an ethical lesson in these lines: that friends ought to endure all things for one another in both words and deeds, and should bring what is lacking in others to a state of fullness by completing their needs. For these are the characteristics of pure and guileless friendship. 

But Iamblichus <note>A later Neoplatonist who emphasized theurgy and divine hierarchy</note>, having once assumed that the "unnamable one" <note>original: "akatonomaston"</note> is superior to those present and a lover of the vision of intelligible realities, says that Socrates indicates through these words that even if the offspring of nature fall short of truly existing beings, they nevertheless draw a certain likeness from them. In the same way, the contemplation that revolves around nature participates somehow in the knowledge of the intelligible realm, and this is what the "filling up" <note>the completion of the group of guests</note> signifies.

->**"Certainly, and to the best of our ability we shall leave nothing out; for it would not be right, after being hosted by you yesterday with such fitting gifts of hospitality, if the rest of us did not eagerly feast you in return." [17 B]**<-

The character of Timaeus is shown through these words to be both proud and temperate, lofty and harmonious, friendly and...

<margin>E</margin>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the dialogue between Socrates and his guests regarding their "feast" of discourse.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B]

...kindly; for the phrase "most certainly indeed" demonstrates both his eagerness regarding the one who is absent and the perfection of his knowledge, according to which he is ready to fill up what is lacking in others, as well as his sincerity. Furthermore, the phrase "to the best of our ability we shall leave nothing undone" sufficiently presents the reliability of his promises and the moderation in his speech concerning himself. These, then, are the **ethical** lessons to be taken from these words.

The **physical** <note>In this context, "physical" refers to the study of nature (physis) and the structural laws of the universe.</note> lessons are that this exchange of discourses bears an image of the communion and reciprocity of powers in the works of nature, through which all things are coordinated and contribute to the single harmony of the whole; and also the fact that the activities of nature change according to time, with different things acting upon different things at different moments. For the act of "feasting in return" the one who provided the feast yesterday is like these natural cycles.

The **theological** lesson is that the demiurgic <note>The "Demiurge" is the creator-god or craftsman of the universe in Platonic thought.</note> cause proceeds through all things and fills all things, cutting off every deficiency through its own power and fertile abundance, according to which it leaves nothing destitute of itself; for it is characterized by being over-full, sufficient, and all-perfect. 

<margin>F</margin> Furthermore, the idea of "feasting in return" is drawn from the banqueting found in divine myths, according to which the gods welcome one another:
->*And they with golden cups pledged one another* <note>Homer, Iliad 4.4</note><-
in the presence of the greatest Zeus, being filled with nectar. And it is not simply said "to feast," but "to feast in return" <note>original: "antaphestian" (ἀνταφεστιᾶν). Proclus emphasizes the prefix 'anti-' (reciprocity) and 'apo-' (completion).</note>; for this "feasting in return" implies the complete fulfillment of the banquet. 

<margin>9</margin> This is also seen in the whole of the cosmos: for the visible arrangements of the world, through their own extreme suitability, call forth the invisible powers, and those powers, through an excess of goodness, bring these arrangements to perfection. All these things are joined to one another, and there is a reciprocal giving of the invitation or a sharing of perfection. Furthermore, the fact that he does all these things "with justice" bears an image of **Justice** <note>Dike, the personification of Justice, often associated with the order of Zeus.</note> who, together with Zeus, arranges all things—

<summary>Proclus interprets the social exchange of speeches in Plato's Timaeus through three lenses: ethical (character), physical (natural reciprocity), and theological (divine abundance and the harmony of the cosmos).</summary>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the symbolic meaning of the "guest-gifts" of discourse and the chronological setting of the dialogue.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B]

...shining forth, while "what is fitting" belongs to the Cause that illuminates the whole with creative beauty, and the "guest-gifts" <note>original: ξένια (xenia) - traditional gifts given to a guest</note> belong to the distinct variation defined according to divine characteristics; for each of the divine beings possesses its own proper powers and activities. Just as Socrates, therefore, entertained Timaeus with the discourses derived from his own philosophy, so too does each of the gods, acting according to their individual powers, contribute toward the single and supreme providence of the Creator <note>original: δημιουργός (demiurgos)</note> over the whole of reality. Let these points be set forth as preliminary exercises in the contemplation of things, appearing through images in the introduction of the work.

<margin>B</margin> From these details, the timing of the dialogues—I mean the *Republic* and the *Timaeus*—is also clear, if indeed the former is set during the festival of Bendis <note>The Bendideia, a festival for the Thracian goddess Bendis</note> celebrated in the Piraeus, and the latter on the day following the Bendideia. For those who have written on the festivals agree that the Bendideia in the Piraeus takes place on the nineteenth of the month of Thargelion <note>roughly May/June</note>; thus the *Timaeus* would be set on the twentieth of that same month. But if, as will be stated further on [26 E ff.], it is also set during the Panathenaea <note>The greatest festival of Athens, honoring Athena</note>, it is clear that this was the Lesser Panathenaea; for the Great Panathenaea took place on the twenty-eighth of the month of Hekatombaion <note>roughly July/August</note>, as has also been recorded by previous historians.

> ->"Do you then remember the magnitude and the nature of the tasks I imposed upon you to discuss?" [17 B]<-

One must first follow the order of the main points. The first of these concerned the number of those completing the gathering; this was followed by the point concerning the replacement of the absent member; and now, thirdly, there is added the point concerning the delivery of the discourses which they were commanded to give. These points are continuous with one another: to the question of *who* should meet corresponds the fact that those who met formed a triad; and to the fact of the triad speaking corresponds the question of *what* they ought to say. In addition to the order, one should observe the precision of the terms...

<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the opening dialogue where Socrates asks his companions if they remember the previous day's discussion regarding the ideal state.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B]

It is proper to consider this: the phrase "you remember" <note>original: *memnēsthe*</note> demonstrates the knowledge shared among the participants in the discourse. For there is also in the Demiurge <note>The "Craftsman" or Creator of the universe in Platonic thought.</note> a memory of all things—a knowledge that is separate, transcendent, and uniform—according to the Memory <note>original: *Mnēmosynē*</note> within him, which is the stable establishment of divine intellection. Even in the secondary gods <note>The lesser deities who assist in creation.</note> there is a subordinate form of intellection, of which the people present here were images. 

<margin>C</margin> And because of this memory pre-existing in the universe, the universal souls are established in the intelligible realms, and the creative principles <note>original: *logoi*</note> possess immutability and motionlessness. Thus, whatever things are deprived of this memory flow away from their own causes, just as partial souls <note>Individual souls, like those of humans.</note> and the natures of generated things do. 

The phrase "how many and concerning what things" is indicative of both the quantity and the quality of beings; these proceed from the total creation, but they also proceed from the more partial gods. As for the word "commanded," if it were said to Critias and Hermocrates, it is clear how it must be referred back to the facts and the principles of world-making. But if it is also said to Timaeus, it is not a symbol of superiority, but a challenge to the intellections within him.

<margin>D</margin> In response to these things, let us observe the answer of Timaeus:

**"Some things we remember; but for those we do not, you being present will remind us." [17 B]**

In these words, one might find an ethical character—as Porphyry <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher and scholar who preceded Proclus.</note> says—which is the mean between irony and boastfulness. For he did not claim to remember everything, nor nothing, but some things and not others. 

There is also a logical aspect: providing a pretext for the summary of the problems, for this is a technique of dialectic. 

There is a physical aspect: that the laws of nature <note>original: *logoi physikoi*</note> both remain forever and yet are in a state of flux, just as the present memory is in one sense preserved and in another sense lost. For what is said regarding the man must also be transferred to the whole of Nature. 

Finally, there is a theological aspect: that the single Demiurge possesses from himself the immutable and the pure...

---

<summary>Proclus interprets Socrates's question about "remembering" as a metaphor for how divine knowledge and creative principles are stabilized in the cosmos. He analyzes Timaeus's humble reply through four lenses: ethical, logical, physical, and theological.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on the opening of Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the symbolic meaning behind Socrates's summary of the Republic.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B]

the one found in the processes of generation, but through the second and third powers she <note>referring to the soul or the governing principle</note> endures as she proceeds, being escorted, as it were, by those powers which pre-establish a calm over the tumult found in things that come to be. Thus, being separate in herself, she is even more so because she has set secondary powers over the things being administered. Furthermore, this "reminding" <note>Greek: *hypomnesis*</note> bears an image of the renewal of the formative principles <term>logoi: the rational, shaping principles that govern the physical world</term> in the universe. For that which flows away from them is called back in a cycle toward the "same" and the "similar," and the order of generation remains unceasing because of the circular motion of the heavens. This motion is always accomplished in the same way because of the Intellect which holds it together and organizes the entire revolution through intellectual powers. It is fitting, then, that Socrates is the one reminding them of these principles, for he is the one who narrated the *Republic*, of which there is a heavenly model.

<margin>10</margin>

-> **"Rather, if it is not too difficult for you, go over them again briefly from the beginning, so that they may be more firmly established among us" [17 B].** <-

<margin>15</margin>

Since the discussion of the Republic was threefold, the first disposition of it was truly difficult because of the sophisticated debates <note>referring to the arguments with Thrasymachus in Book I of the Republic</note>; the second was easier than the one before it; and the third was the easiest, containing the entire form of the Republic in a condensed way. This repetition is appropriate for natural things because of the rebirth <term>palingenesia: the recurring cycle of birth and renewal</term> within them and the recycling back to the same form. It is from this cycle that the forms remain stable in the world; their flowing away and their loss are called back by the recycling, which is also why the heaven is in eternal motion, and by unfolding many periods, it returns to the same life. But for what reason did he not mention these things—neither the persons nor the promises—in the narration of the *Republic*, yet here he adds them? Because in the whole of reality, the models <term>paradeigmata: the eternal archetypes or Forms</term> have already embraced all—

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B-C]

the accounts of the images, but the images themselves are not strong enough to encompass the entire power of their causes. Just as, therefore, a memory was made in the second gathering of the persons present in the first, so in the third gathering he recalls those things passed over in silence in the second; for effects can be contemplated more perfectly within their superior causes. You might also say, from a theological perspective, that while Timaeus—standing in analogy to the whole <term>Demiurgy</term><note>The act of world-creation or the work of the Divine Creator.</note>—has embraced everything: the persons, the promises, and the discourses themselves; Socrates in the *Republic*, being ranked in analogy to the simplicity of the triple creation, weaves only the form of the state, which is heavenly. Here, then, as in a single, all-perfect Living Being, all things are embraced: the first, the middle, and the last, and the entire unfolding of the whole.

But how and for what reason is the "Republic" presented for a third time? <note>original: "τρίτον" (triton). Proclus is discussing why the summary of the Republic appears here at the start of the Timaeus.</note> It is because the life of the soul is threefold: the first is that which restrains the <term>Logos</term><note>Reason or rational principle.</note> and orders it by justice and directs it as is necessary; the second is that which is turned toward itself and desires to think for itself according to its own inherent justice; and the third is that which ascends toward its causes and establishes its own activities within them. 

Furthermore, to speak "briefly" <note>original: "διὰ βραχέων" (dia bracheon)</note> bears an image of the life coiled up into the One Mind and of the intellectual encompassing of all things. And the phrase "go over it again" <note>original: "ἐπάνελθε" (epanelthe)</note> provides a wonderful indication of the collective perfection that leads back to the highest end, and of a more eternal—if you wish to call it so—intellection. For this is what it means to be "more firmly established": to hold a more stable and eternal grasp of the same things.

> **"Yesterday, the chief point of the discourse I delivered concerning the state was what kind of state, and consisting of what kind of men, would appear to me to be the best." [17 B-C]**

<meta>This page continues the introductory commentary by Proclus on Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing why the dialogue begins with a summary of the Republic before moving into natural philosophy.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B-C]

Some say they <note>the speakers in the dialogue</note> are indicating to us that those who grasp the contemplation of the whole must first have their characters ordered. Others, however, claim that this summary is set forth as an image of the arrangement of the universe and of the entirety of "physiology" <note>original: "physiologia" — here meaning the study of nature or natural philosophy</note>. For it was a custom among the Pythagoreans, prior to scientific instruction, to present the disclosure of the subjects under investigation through similarities and images, <margin>C</margin> and after this to introduce the secret indication of these same matters through symbols. Then, in this way, after the awakening of the soul’s intellect and the purification of its eye, they would bring forward the complete scientific knowledge of the proposed subjects.

And so here, the abbreviated tradition of the *Republic* <note>original: "politeia" — the social and political order</note> provided before the study of nature establishes us, by way of an image, in the craftsmanship of the universe; while the history concerning the Atlantic people <note>the story of Atlantis</note> does so symbolically. For myths, for the most part, are accustomed to indicate realities through symbols. Thus, the study of nature extends through the entire dialogue, but in different ways according to the various modes of tradition. These, then, are the points concerning the purpose of the words set before us.

That it was reasonable for the summary of the *Republic* to occur in this treatise can be gathered from many sources. For the art of politics exists, as we learned in the *Protagoras* **[321 D]**, and true virtue shines out within this world. Therefore, <margin>D</margin> Timaeus **[34 B]** says that the world is known to itself and a friend to itself because of virtue. Furthermore, since there are three types of political order and the primary one is assigned to the universal craftsmanship <note>original: "demiurgia"</note>—as we demonstrate in other works—it is reasonable that the form of this order is handed down here in a most concise way, where the goal is to contemplate the universal Creator <note>original: "demiurgos"</note> generating and arranging the whole. It is possible to work out these points at greater length; but let us return to the text and the words of the...

<meta>This page continues the commentary by Proclus on Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the introductory summary of the Republic provided by Socrates.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 BC]

Socrates. <note>original: "Σωκράτους"</note> Among these matters, there is much dispute among the commentators, who write and counter-write against one another regarding a single punctuation mark <note>original: "στιγμῆς"; referring to where a sentence ends in the Greek text</note> and, based on this punctuation, explain the aim of the *Republic* in various ways. For some, having placed the punctuation at the word "republic," define its aim according to the title, citing Plato himself as a witness concerning the "republic." Others, however, at the phrase "of the discourses," declare that the aim is concerning justice, and that this section concerning the republic was introduced as a specific heading of the things said for the sake of justice. If one is not to talk nonsense in speaking and counter-speaking, it must be said that both views somehow coincide with one another. For the <margin>E</margin> discourse concerning justice is about the internal republic <note>The "inner city" or constitution of the soul</note>; for it rightly arranges the communion of the powers within us. And the discourse concerning the republic is about justice as it occurs in a multitude. Both, therefore, come to the same thing: the same reality is "justice" in the soul, "republic" in the city, and "orderliness" <note>original: "κοσμιότης"</note> in the cosmos. One ought not to have trouble by dividing from one another things that are joined by nature. So much for that.

Longinus and Origen <note>A Neoplatonist contemporary of Plotinus, not the Christian father</note>, however, dispute with one another from a different starting point: concerning which kind of republic Socrates is discussing in these passages—whether the first or the middle. For they see that the republic there lives naturally, martially, and intellectually. Longinus, on the one hand, thinks the discourse here is about the middle republic, because he calls the auxiliaries <term>auxiliaries: ἐπικούρους, the warrior class in Plato's ideal city</term> "guardians" and says the guardians are those who will go to war. Origen, on the other hand, thinks it is about the first republic; for in this one, he delivers the branches of learning to the guardians. But we shall say to them as well that one ought not to tear apart the single republic, nor divide the continuity of <margin>F</margin> life from itself. For there is one republic, perfecting itself and growing together by more perfect additions. For the whole possesses the "natural" in the serfs <term>serfs: θησί, the laboring class</term>, the "martial" in the auxiliaries, and the "intellectual" in the guardians. So that concerning...

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus’s commentary on the opening of Plato’s Timaeus, specifically discussing the relationship between the "Republic" (Politeia) and the study of nature (Physiology).</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 B-C]

The discourse concerns the whole state <note>original: "politeia" — referring here to the ideal social and cosmic order described in Plato's Republic</note>, and one should not dispute these matters, but rather consider how it is reasonable to call the "Republic" both inferior to and superior to the study of nature <note>original: "physiologia" — the study of the physical world and its origins</note>. For insofar as it deals with human affairs as its matter <margin>11</margin> <margin>5</margin> and aims to bring order to them, it holds a second rank behind the study of nature and is more partial; but insofar as it subsists in universal principles and is organized in an incorporeal and immaterial way, it is superior and more total. For indeed, the cosmos is a kind of state, yet it is also partial, because every body is partial. In general, the "Republic" pre-exists <margin>10</margin> in the Intelligible realm, it exists also in the heavens, and finally, it exists in human lives. Thus, if it is superior to the physical creation, it rightly precedes the *Timaeus*; if it is inferior—because the former is a moral world while the latter is a cosmic and complete world—it is reasonable that we should need to ascend from lower things <margin>15</margin> to more venerable ones. Both views are true for the reasons stated. Since, as we said, the form of the "Republic" was both universal and sketched out upon partial matter, for this reason Socrates took up the "what sort" for the sake of the form, and the "from what sort of men" for the sake of the matter.

<margin>20</margin> > **"And indeed, Socrates, it was spoken to us all**
> **according to Mind." [17 C]** <note>The Greek "kata noun" can mean "to our liking" or "according to Intellect/Mind." Proclus interprets it in the higher philosophical sense.</note>

The guidance "according to Mind," rather than according to pleasure or mere opinion, indicates a marvelous perfection and an intellectual <margin>B</margin> application of the arguments; and before this, it hints at the harmonious meeting of all secondary <margin>25</margin> causes into one Mind and one unified creation. Furthermore, the addition of the word "indeed" <note>original: "mala" — meaning "very much" or "certainly"</note> reveals the surpassing union through which all the creative causes converge toward a single center: the one paternal cause of all things.

<summary>Proclus explains why the "Republic" serves as a necessary introduction to the "Timaeus," arguing that the ideal state exists on three levels: the divine/intelligible, the celestial, and the human. He interprets the characters' agreement as a symbol of cosmic harmony and the unification of all causes under a single divine Mind.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the commentary of Proclus on the opening dialogue of Plato's Timaeus, specifically addressing the summary of the Republic. It establishes the Neoplatonic method of reading political structures as symbols of cosmic order.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 C]
### PROCLUS ed. Diehl. I 3

"Did we not, then, first separate the class of husbandmen <note>original: γεωργῶν; referring to the laboring class</note> and all other crafts in it from the class of those who were to fight in defense?" <insert>[17 C. cf. Republic II 369 D ff]</insert>

The discourse concerning the Republic and the coiled <note>original: συνεσπειραμένη; meaning tightly wound or concentrated</note> and brief recapitulation of the classes within it contributes to the entire tradition of the cosmic demiurgy <note>term: demiurgy; the process of world-making by a divine Creator</note>. For it is possible to ascend from these things, as if from images, to the wholes. Indeed, the Pythagoreans pursued this very thing with distinction, hunting for the similarities of beings through analogies and passing from images to the archetypes <note>original: παραδείγματα; the eternal patterns or "blueprints" of reality</note>. 

This is exactly what Plato is doing now, showing us the things in the universe by means of human lives and allowing us to contemplate them before we set out on the main path of the argument. And this is not surprising; for the political systems of virtuous men are made in the likeness of the heavenly arrangement. Therefore, we too must refer these current images to the wholes, and first of all, the matter concerning the division of the classes. 

For this very cutting <note>original: τομὴ; a technical term for logical or ontological division</note> of the classes imitates the demiurgic division in the cosmos, according to which it is neither possible for incorporeal things to change into the nature of bodies, nor do bodies depart from their own essence toward an incorporeal subsistence. By this same principle, mortal things remain mortal and immortal things stand eternally without fail, and their various ranks have their archetypal causes pre-existing in the wholes. 

For if you should wish to arrange the entire city in analogy to the whole cosmos—since man is surely a "small cosmos" <note>term: microcosm; the idea that the human reflects the universe</note>, and is not a city also a small cosmos?—and if you should cut the whole city into two, the "upper city" and the "lower city," and associate the one with Heaven and the other with the realm of Generation <note>term: Generation; the physical world of change, birth, and decay</note>, you would certainly find the analogy to be fitting. 

Furthermore, according to the division into three, you might take in the city the mortal element <note>the laborers</note>, the warrior element, and the guardian element; just as in the soul you find the appetitive part, which provides for the needs of the body, and the spirited part, which...

<summary>Proclus explains that Plato's summary of the Republic serves as a symbolic prelude to the Timaeus, where the structure of the ideal city mirrors the eternal structure of the universe. He introduces the Pythagorean method of using analogies to understand the relationship between the human soul, the state, and the cosmos.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on Plato's Timaeus (specifically 17 C), where he draws analogies between the classes of the ideal city and the structure of the soul and the cosmos.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 C]

is appointed to repel whatever is destructive to the living being, acting as a bodyguard to the 
ruling principle within us <note>original: "τὸ ἐν ἡμῖν ἄρχον" — referring to the rational faculty</note>, and the rational part, which is philosophical by 
essence and kingly over our entire life; while in the whole 
multitude of souls, it is the part that serves the realm of generation, 
<margin>5</margin>ministering to the cosmic providences of the gods, which is led back 
toward the Intelligible; and in all worldly things simply, it is the 
mortal race, the demonic tribe, and the order of the celestial gods. For 
these latter are truly the guardians and saviors of the 
whole, while the demons in turn lead the way for their creative work 
<margin>10</margin>and restrain all discord within the cosmos. 
There is also a certain natural providence for mortals, which both 
generates and maintains them according to the Divine Mind. Furthermore, according to another 
division, the agricultural class of the city is analogous to Selene <note>The Moon</note> 
who encompasses the laws of generative nature; 
<margin>15</margin>the class overseeing common marriages is analogous to Aphrodite, the cause of all 
<margin>E</margin>harmony and the union of male with female and form with 
matter; the class providing for the clever distribution of lots 
is analogous to Hermes, both because of the lots, over which the god presides, 
and because of the craftiness inherent in them; the educational and 
<margin>20</margin>judicial class is analogous to Helios <note>The Sun</note>, with whom dwell worldly Justice, 
the Uplifter, and the Seven-Rayed God <note>original: "Ἑπτάκτις" — a title for the Sun in the Chaldean Oracles</note> according to the theologians; the 
warrior class is analogous to the order of Ares, which presides over all 
oppositions in the cosmos and the "otherness" of the whole; the 
kingly class is analogous to Zeus, the provider of leading wisdom and of practical and 
<margin>25</margin>ordering intellect; and the philosophical class is analogous to Kronos, insofar 
as he is intellectual and ascends even unto the First Cause. These 
matters must therefore be understood in this way through analogies. It 
seems that Plato divides the city in two, setting as one class the 
agricultural and the technical—which is indeed called "demiurgic" <note>craft-working</note>—and as 
<margin>30</margin>another the warrior class in the upper city; not because he is now 
summarizing a "warlike" constitution, as Longinus says,

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 CD]

...but because through the term "those who fight in the front," he included both the auxiliaries <note>The 'auxiliaries' are the warrior class in Plato's ideal city, supporting the rulers.</note> and the guardians; for some fight in the front with their hands, and others with their minds. Just as among the Greeks,
<margin>F</margin> Ajax fought as the "bulwark of the Achaeans," <note>original: "ἕρκος τῶν Ἀχαιῶν" — a Homeric epithet for Ajax the Great.</note> but
<margin>5</margin> Nestor also fought as the "watcher of the Achaeans," <note>original: "οὖρος τῶν Ἀχαιῶν" — Nestor is the aged, wise counselor.</note> the latter warding off the enemy through his counsels as a guardian, while the former used his hands against them. Unless, perhaps, he specifically mentions
those who fight in the front now because he wishes to nar-
rate the military deeds of such a state.
<margin>10</margin> **"And having assigned, according to nature, to each person the one single occupation that was appropriate to them, we said that these, above all, must be the ones to go to war"** [17 CD. cf. Republic III 394 E].
First, the phrasing must be read in two ways: either that
<margin>15</margin> we gave one occupation to each of the citizens **according to nature**, so that each might perform his own proper work; or that we gave to each the task of practicing the occupation that is **according to nature**, which suits each person
according to their existing natural aptitude. Next, one must
<margin>20</margin> seek the reason why Socrates defined it thus, or the reason he gave himself: for every occupation is practiced correctly both by the one who is naturally suited to it and by the one who applies themselves to the practice with care. For neither can care, when deprived of natural aptitude, achieve anything perfect, nor does talent proceed into activity
<margin>25</margin> without care. The end result, therefore, comes from both. But if this is so, a person practicing many tasks would not be able to be naturally suited to all of them equally, nor to care for all of them equally, being divided by his zeal for many things. If these qualities are diminished, it is necessary that the
<margin>30</margin> occupations of the citizens appear more mediocre. If this is not <margin>B</margin>
right, one occupation must be distributed to each of the citizens,
toward which he is naturally suited, and his care must be directed toward that one thing...

<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the division of labor in the ideal state (Tim. 17C-D) and how this earthly hierarchy mirrors the divine order of the cosmos.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17C-D]

...to exert every effort; for one who is naturally gifted for a particular way of life and who pursues what is in accordance with their nature with diligence will, in all likelihood, become the best at their own specific work. Now, in the case of human political systems, it is easy to observe such a division, for our nature is fragmented. But how can this be true in the case of the gods? <margin>5</margin> For the divine is all-powerful and all-perfect.

Or must we also say of the gods that "all things are in all things," <note>A famous Neoplatonic principle: everything exists in everything else, but in a manner appropriate to the nature of the participant.</note> but each is all things according to its own specific character and possesses the cause of all things—one in the manner of Helios <note>The Sun god</note>, another in the manner of Hermes? For starting from the divine **henads** <term>Henads: the "unities" or highest gods that stand above even Being itself</term>, this specific character descends through the **intellective substances** <margin>10</margin> <term>Intellective substances: the realm of pure Mind or Nous</term>, through the divine souls, and through the bodies themselves. Because of this, some of these entities participate in a **demiurgic** <term>Demiurgic: the power of "crafting" or organizing the material world</term> power, others in a generative power, others in a cohesive power, and others in a distributive power, and they act upon the world of becoming in this way. <margin>15</margin>

In the gods themselves, this specific character pre-exists, defining the henads according to the "infinitude" found there and the "divine dyad" <note>The principles of Limit and Boundlessness that generate all reality</note>. <margin>C</margin> In the **Intellects**, "otherness" distinguishes the wholes from the parts, distributing the intellective powers and providing a unique rank to each, through which the purity of the Minds is not confused. <margin>20</margin> In the souls, there is progression and division, with different souls following different lives, <insert>according to which</insert> some have been allotted a divine existence, others an angelic, others a daemonic, and others a different kind. In bodies, there is spatial extension, instilling different powers in different things. For even in these bodies, there are the final reflections of the **intelligible** <margin>25</margin> <term>Intelligible: the highest realm of reality, accessible only to the mind, not the senses</term> realities, according to which this thing is productive of that, and one thing is in **sympathy** <term>Sympathy: the "hidden strings" or natural resonance connecting different parts of the universe</term> with another, while something else holds this sympathy toward another.

Just as each thing in the universe performs its function according to the nature for which it was stationed by the **Demiurgy** <note>The divine act of creation/ordering</note>, so too in the city the occupations of each person are divided, <margin>30</margin> and each person is assigned to that for which they are naturally suited. What, then, are the works of the "defenders" <note>The guardian class in Plato's Republic</note>? He clarifies this himself in what follows, saying:

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically focusing on the summary of the Republic's social classes as they relate to the cosmic order. Proclus is interpreting the role of the "guardians" mentioned in Timaeus 17D-18A.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17D 18A]

That they should be guardians only of the city, whether someone from without or even from within should come to do harm; acting as judges, on the one hand, with mildness toward those ruled by them who are friends by nature, <margin>D</margin> but becoming harsh, <margin>5</margin> on the other hand, in battles against those of the enemy whom they encounter [17D 18A. cf. Republic III 415D ff, II 375B-C]. In these lines, he wishes the guardians and the <term>auxiliaries</term><note>The 'epikouroi' or helpers, the secondary class in Plato's Republic responsible for defense.</note> to be judges of those within, should anyone harm the city, and combatants against those without—the auxiliaries in one way, <margin>10</margin> and the guardians in another, as was said previously [p. 35, 1 ff]. To be guardians "only" is not a reduction of their power; for even when we speak of the <term>First</term><note>The One, the supreme principle in Neoplatonism.</note>, saying that it is "only One," we do not diminish it or shut it up into something altogether narrow. For that which possesses the best "only" is not diminished by this; <margin>15</margin> on the contrary, every addition to such a thing is a diminution. Thus, the "not only" in these cases diminishes the whole, not the "only"; for by however much you increase the quantity, by so much do you diminish the original principle. These matters are as such. But again, we must consider how the things now being said are observed in the <margin>20</margin> <term>Wholes</term><note>The universal or cosmic levels of reality.</note>. For what is "without" in the universe? And how does the All not encompass all things? Either evil exists in the world in two ways—in souls and in bodies—and it is necessary for those who remove discord and disorder from the All to extend justice and measure to the souls, but to struggle against the <margin>25</margin> instability of <term>matter</term><note>Hyle; the underlying substrate of the physical world, often associated with disorder.</note>. For souls belong by nature to the <term>Intelligible</term><note>The realm of true Being and Forms, accessible to the mind.</note>, <margin>E</margin> which is why they might be said to be "from within" and from the [the] breadth of the Intelligible All; but things that are material and far from the gods are in a way alien to them, both foreign and "from without." Therefore, the fulfillers of Justice deal mildly with the former as being friends by nature, <margin>30</margin> but they are harsh toward the discordant and disordered elements of bodies.

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the divine protection of the cosmos and the nature of the guardian class as described in the Republic.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 17 D – 18 A]

One should understand it in this way: the discordant and disordered flow of bodies occurs sometimes due to the weakness of the <term>logoi</term> <note>original: "λόγων"; here referring to the formative principles or "rational blueprints" that shape matter</note>, and sometimes due to the excess of <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλης"; the raw, unformed substance of the universe</note>. The formative principles are akin to the productive causes, while matter—because of its own indeterminacy and its distance from the source of being—is foreign to the forces that adorn it. Therefore, the unopposable strength of the gods, the unchangeable order, and the guardianship of the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>The "Craftsman" or creator god in Plato's philosophy</note>, by completely removing discord, renews the formative principles and comforts <margin>B</margin> their weakness. It conquers the excess of matter—not because matter actively resists the gods who produce it, but because, in its indeterminacy, it flees from order. It is held fast by the <term>Forms</term> <note>original: "εἴδεσι"; the perfect archetypes of all things</note> through the demiurgic guardianship, which nothing can resist. All things must be obedient to this guardianship so that everything in the cosmos may endure forever, and so that the Demiurge may be the father of eternal things, being himself unmoved and established separately in eternity.

**"For we said, I believe, that a certain nature of the guardians of the soul must be at once spirited and, to a high degree, philosophical, so that they might be gentle to some and harsh to others" [18 A. cf. Republic II 375 C – 376 C].**

The "philosophical" and the "spirited" qualities encompass both classes: the auxiliary and that which is specifically called the "guardian" class, just as the "appetitive" quality will apply to the third class, called the "laboring" class. Because he <note>Plato</note> divided the upper city from the lower, he indicated the differences of the ranks within it by these two names. It is as if someone, dividing the cosmos into heaven and the realm of generation, said that in heaven there are both <margin>C</margin> demonic and divine ranks, and called both of them "guardians" of generation and of the whole. For the whole is guarded by gods and is also guarded by <term>daemons</term> <note>original: "δαιμόνων"; intermediary spirits between gods and mortals, not "demons" in the modern evil sense</note>—by the former in a universal, unified, and transcendent way, and by the latter...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page discussing the divine hierarchy and the roles of the guardians in the ideal city as reflections of cosmic powers.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 18 A]

...but in a partial and multiplied way, and more closely to those things guarded by them. For around every god is stationed a multitude of <term>daimones</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, daimones are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, acting as executors of divine will.</note> who distribute that god's single and total providence. <margin>5</margin> It is fitting, then, that the "philosophical" quality belongs to the gods insofar as they are united with the <term>Intelligible</term> <note>The realm of pure Being and Truth.</note> and filled with Being; whereas the "spirited" quality <note>The *thymoeides*, or the part of the soul associated with courage and righteous anger.</note> belongs to the daimones insofar as they restrain every discord from the universe and act as bodyguards to the gods—just as spirit acts for reason—and insofar as they are the saviors of the divine laws and the decrees of <term>Adrasteia</term> <note>A goddess of inevitable destiny or necessity, often identified with Nemesis.</note>. <margin>10</margin> For these reasons, they are gentle to those who belong to them, harmoniously consoling their weakness as being kin by nature, but harsh to those from without, as they eliminate their indeterminacy in an exempt manner and through the highest superiority.

<margin>D</margin>
<margin>15</margin>
-> *And what of their upbringing? Must they not be nourished in all respects by gymnastics and music, and by the branches of learning that are appropriate to these?* <margin>[18 A. cf. Republic III 401 ff]</margin> <-

The arguments already stated were certain "types" or patterns extending to all in common, according to the demiurgic allotment and divine difference, defining the appropriate pursuits for each and distributing powers suitably to those <margin>20</margin> receiving them. From this point forward, the life of the citizens is woven together, proceeding from the beginning to the end as is necessary through upbringing, pursuits, associations, and the procreation of children. What, then, is this "upbringing," and how is it likened to the Whole? For in the city, there is a certain education of the soul that regulates <margin>25</margin> the irrational part through music and gymnastics; the former relaxing the tension of the spirit, and the latter awakening desire and rendering it harmonious and symmetrical with the spirit, when it has become too dissolved through its descent into the material...

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's commentary on Plato's Timaeus, specifically discussing the education of the "guardians" of the universe by drawing parallels to the Republic.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON THE TIMAEUS I [Tim. 18 A]

...descent and filled from that source with a lack of life, while the rational discourse—through those branches of study <note>original: "mathemata"</note> which possess a certain drawing power and a capacity to remind us of true Being, and which lead the intellectual part within us up toward the brightest aspect of Being—is clear to those who have not entirely fallen into a state of forgetfulness <margin>E</margin> <margin>5</margin> regarding the things ordained in that higher realm. But what the "nourishment" is in the context of the whole universe, and what "music" and "gymnastics" are, and what the "studies" of the guardians of the universe consist of, it is now our task to seek. Perhaps, then, we might say that "nourishment" is the perfection that fulfills each being with the goods appropriate to it and makes it self-sufficient <margin>10</margin> according to both its intellections and its acts of providence; while "music" and "gymnastics" are those powers which, on the one hand, render lives harmonious, and on the other, produce a divine movement that is rhythmic and melodic, always maintaining the same form and the same unchangeable state of the divine vehicles <note>The "vehicles" (ochemeta) are the subtle bodies or astral shells that carry the soul.</note>. It is for these reasons, indeed, that <margin>15</margin> elsewhere he <note>Plato</note> called the divine souls "Sirens" <note>A reference to the Myth of Er in the Republic, where Sirens sit upon the celestial spheres.</note> and declared the heavenly movement to be rhythmic. For "gymnastics" exists among those higher beings, whereas "medicine" exists among the things beneath the moon, which are subject to what is contrary to nature. Perhaps, then, if we were to say these things, we would be speaking correctly. For from above, starting from the Intelligible realm, powers reach <margin>20</margin> down to the whole heaven, shining upon their lives the best harmony and upon their vehicles an unchangeable strength. Since even the "studies" <note>mathemata</note> are the intellections of the heavenly souls, <margin>F</margin> according to which they run back up to the Intelligible, following the great Zeus and beholding both the unitary Number and the truly <margin>25</margin> existing Heaven and the intellectual Shape. Therefore, one might say that within them exists the truest Arithmetic, Astronomy, and Geometry; for they behold "absolute speed" and "absolute slowness," the paradigms of the periods in the heavens.
